1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Product: Mosunetuzumab (LUNSUMIO)
Pharmacological Class: Bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager.
Proposed Indication: Mosunetuzumab is a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager indicated
for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after at least
two prior systemic therapies.
Dosing regimen: The recommended dosage of mosunetuzumab is 1 mg on Cycle 1, Day 1; 2 mg
on Cycle 1 Day 8; 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1; and 30 mg on Cycle 3 Day 1
onward, administered every 3 weeks.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The FDA review team recommends accelerated approval of mosunetuzumab for the following
indication:
Mosunetuzumab is a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager indicated for the treatment of
adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of
systemic therapy.
This recommendation is based on the efficacy of mosunetuzumab from GO29781, an openlabel, multicenter, multicohort study in 90 patients with relapsed or refractory follicular
lymphoma (FL) who had received at least two prior therapies, including an anti-CD20
monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent.
Patients received step-up doses of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, followed
by 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 60 mg on Cycle 2 Day 1, then 30 mg every 3 weeks in
subsequent cycles. A treatment cycle was 21 days. Mosunetuzumab was administered for 8
cycles unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. After 8 cycles,
patients with a complete response discontinued therapy; patients with a partial response or
stable disease continued treatment up to 17 cycles, unless patients experienced progressive
disease or unacceptable toxicity.
In the 90 patients with relapsed or refractory FL enrolled in the GO29781 study, the median age
was 60 years (range: 29 to 90 years), 33% were 65 years of age or older, 61% were male, 82%
17
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
were White, 9% were Asian, 4% were Black or African American, and 8% were Hispanic or
Latino. A total of 77% of patients had Stage III-IV disease, 34% had bulky disease, and all
patients had an Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of 0 or 1. The
median number of prior therapies was 3 (range: 2 to 10), with 38% receiving 2 prior therapies,
31% receiving 3 prior therapies, and 31% receiving more than 3 prior therapies.
Efficacy was established on the basis of objective response rate (ORR) and duration of response
(DOR) as assessed by an independent review facility (IRF) according to standard criteria for NHL
(Cheson 2007). Among the 90 patients, the ORR per IRF was 80% (95% CI: 70, 88), with 60%
achieving a complete response. With a median follow-up for DOR of 14.9 months, the
estimated median DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 10, not reached). Of the 72 responders, 62%
remained in response at 12 months and 57% at 18 months.
In this population, the overall response rate of 80%, with 60% achieving complete response,
with durability and a tolerable safety profile constitutes a meaningful clinical benefit. To
support accelerated approval, the efficacy data are considered in the context of available
therapy for the patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting
and beyond. Available therapy includes anti-CD20 therapy (i.e., rituximab and obinutuzumab)
as a single agent and in combination, bendamustine monotherapy and in combination, and
immunotherapy combinations such as rituximab plus lenalidomide. In the 3rd line setting and
beyond, the ORRs for these therapies, including single-agent and combinations, ranges from
57% to 80% with associated durability. In the 90 patients with FL treated with mosunetuzumab,
all patients received prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent and the
median number of prior therapies was 3. A total of 79% of patients were refractory to prior
anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody therapy, 53% were refractory to both anti-CD20 monoclonal
antibody and alkylator therapy, 9% received prior rituximab plus lenalidomide therapy, 21%
received prior autologous stem cell transplant, and 3% received prior CAR T therapy. Given the
prior therapies received, the overall response rate of 80% with associated durability for
mosunetuzumab provides data to support a clinically meaningful benefit in the context of
available therapy for patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line
setting and beyond.
Thus, the efficacy data for mosunetuzumab demonstrates substantial evidence of effectiveness
in support of accelerated approval for the following indication:
• Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or
more lines of systemic therapy

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)

Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
The benefit-risk assessment is favorable for mosunetuzumab, a bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager indicated for the treatment of adult
patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.
Efficacy:
Efficacy of mosunetuzumab is based on the results from GO29781, an open-label, multicenter, multicohort study in 90 patients with relapsed or
refractory follicular lymphoma who had received at least two prior therapies, including an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating
agent.
Patients received step-up doses of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, followed by 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 60 mg on Cycle 2
Day 1, then 30 mg every 3 weeks in subsequent cycles. A treatment cycle was 21 days. Mosunetuzumab was administered for 8 cycles unless
patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. After 8 cycles, patients with a complete response discontinued therapy;
patients with a partial response or stable disease continued treatment up to 17 cycles, unless patients experienced progressive disease or
unacceptable toxicity.
Efficacy was established on the basis of objective response rate (ORR) and duration of response (DOR) as assessed by an independent review
facility (IRF) according to standard criteria for NHL (Cheson 2007). Among the 90 patients, the ORR per IRF was 80% (95% CI: 70, 88), with 60%
achieving a complete response. With a median follow-up for DOR of 14.9 months, the estimated median DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 10, not
reached). Of the 72 responders, 62% remained in response at 12 months and 57% at 18 months.
Safety:
The safety profile of mosunetuzumab was primarily supported by analysis of 218 patients with B-cell NHL that received the recommended dose
and regimen (1/2/60/30 mg). The median duration of treatment was 8 cycles (range 1 to 17), with 52% exposed for at least 8 cycles. The most
common adverse events (≥20%) were cytokine release syndrome (39%), fatigue (36%), rash (34%), pyrexia (24%), and headache (21%). The
most common Grade 3 to 4 laboratory abnormalities (≥ 10%) were decreased lymphocyte count (92%), decreased phosphate (41%), increased
glucose (40%), decreased neutrophil count (38%), increased uric acid (15%), decreased white blood cell count (22%), decreased hemoglobin
19
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

positions of the FDA.

(19%), and decreased platelets (12%). Serious adverse events occurred in 52%, most often due to CRS (21%), pneumonia (5%), pyrexia (5%),
sepsis (3%), and a 2% incidence each of tumor flare, urinary tract infection, pleural effusion, and renal insufficiency. Adverse events leading to
treatment interruption occurred in 33%, most often due to neutropenia, infection, and CRS. Adverse events leading to treatment
discontinuation occurred in 4%, most often due to CRS. Thus, the primary safety issues identified with mosunetuzumab include CRS, neurologic
toxicity (including immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome [ICANS]), infections, cytopenias, and tumor flare.
Benefit-Risk:
Mosunetuzumab has an overall favorable benefit-risk in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of
systemic therapy. In the 90 patients with relapsed or refractory FL enrolled in the GO29781 study, the median age was 60 years (range: 29 to 90
years), 33% were 65 years of age or older, 61% were male, 82% were White, 9% were Asian, 4% were Black or African American, and 8% were
Hispanic or Latino. A total of 77% of patients had Stage III-IV disease, 34% had bulky disease, and all patients had an Eastern Cooperative
Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of 0 or 1. The median number of prior therapies was 3 (range: 2 to 10), with 38% receiving 2 prior
therapies, 31% receiving 3 prior therapies, and 31% receiving more than 3 prior therapies.
In this population, the overall response rate of 80%, with 60% achieving complete response, with durability and a tolerable safety profile
constitutes a meaningful clinical benefit and a favorable benefit-risk evaluation. To support accelerated approval, the efficacy data are
considered in the context of available therapy for the patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and
beyond. Available therapy includes anti-CD20 therapy (i.e., rituximab and obinutuzumab) as a single agent and in combination, bendamustine
monotherapy and in combination, and immunotherapy combinations such as rituximab plus lenalidomide. In the 3rd line setting and beyond,
the ORRs for these therapies, including single-agent and combinations, ranges from 57% to 80% with associated durability. In the 90 patients
with FL, the median number of prior therapies was 3 and included an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent. A total of 79% of
patients were refractory to prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody therapy, 53% were refractory to both anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and
alkylator therapy, 9% received prior rituximab plus lenalidomide therapy, 21% received prior autologous stem cell transplant, and 3% received
prior CAR T therapy. Therefore, the overall response of 80% with associated durability for mosunetuzumab provides data to support a clinically
meaningful benefit in the context of available therapy for patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and
beyond.
Thus, the benefit-risk of mosunetuzumab is deemed favorable to support accelerated approval for the following indication:
• Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Follicular lymphoma (FL) is the second most common non-Hodgkin lymphoma (NHL),
accounting for an estimated 13,960 new cases diagnosed in the US in 2016 (Teras et al. 2016).
In the US, FL is more frequently diagnosed in White (~92%) than in Black (∼4%) or Asian/Pacific
Islander (∼4%) racial groups (Li et al. 2015; Blansky et al. 2020), while the incidence rates in men
and women are similar. The incidence increases with age, and the median age of diagnosis of FL
in the US is 63 years (SEER Cancer Statistics 2020).
FL tumor cells are malignant counterparts of normal germinal center B cells. In 85% of cases,
cytogenetic analysis reveals the genetic hallmark of t(14;18)(q32;q21) genomic translocation,
which results in the constitutive overexpression of the BCL-2 protein, a member of a family of
proteins that regulates apoptosis. Almost all cases of FL carry additional genetic alterations
(Hiddemann and Cheson 2014). The FL immunophenotype usually shows CD10+, CD20+, CD19+,
CD22+ CD5- cells expressing surface immunoglobulin.
The high degree of mutational heterogeneity in FL is reflected in a highly variable clinical
behavior, ranging from an extremely indolent course over decades to a clinically highly
aggressive course with short DOR after treatment and recurrence of disease within 1-2 years
after initial treatment (Freedman and Jacobsen 2020). Histologic transformation of FL from an
indolent disease to the more aggressive DLBCL occurs with a risk of 2−3% per year, and is
associated with more aggressive disease progression and poor survival outcome (Montoto and
Fitzgibbon 2011; Link et al. 2013). Typically, the clinical course of FL is characterized by a series
of remissions and relapses, with generally increasing refractoriness and decreasing duration of
response to therapy. As the disease recurs, patients are treated with multiple lines of therapies
during their lifetime. FL remains incurable, and a significant proportion of patients die from
recurrent disease or treatment-related toxicity.
Patients with FL typically present with asymptomatic lymphadenopathy, however the majority
of patients are diagnosed with advanced disease (Ann Arbor Stage III/IV), with up to 70% with
bone marrow involvement (Freedman and Jacobsen 2020). The Follicular Lymphoma
International Prognostic Index (FLIPI) is the most widely used prognostic scoring system to
predict survival in newly diagnosed patients, though it may be prognostic at first relapse as well
(Montoto et al. 2004).
Observation (watch-and-wait) is the standard practice for patients with advanced stage low
24
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
tumor burden FL. Guidelines recommend that treatment is only initiated upon occurrence of
one or more of the modified GELF criteria (Brice et al. 1997; NCCN Guidelines v4.2021). For
patients with FL for whom initiating systemic treatment is indicated, immunochemotherapy
with an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody (rituximab or obinutuzumab) and chemotherapy
(bendamustine, CHOP or CVP), with or without extended maintenance dosing with rituximab or
obinutuzumab monotherapy for up to 2 years is the standard of care for first-line treatment.
After initiating treatment, quality of response (e.g., achieving a CR, PCR-negative MRD in blood
or bone marrow, or negative PET at end of treatment) is associated with improved OS (Bachy et
al. 2010; Trotman et al 2018). A significantly higher risk of death is observed in patients with
early progression of disease, specifically within 24 months of commencing first-line
immunochemotherapy (Casulo et al. 2017; Seymour et al. 2019). Beyond the front-line setting,
prognosis is influenced by several factors, including number of prior regimens, refractory status,
and progressive decline of bone marrow reserve (Smith 2013). In patients with FL who progress
from front-line therapies, the disease-free intervals and DOR become progressively shorter with
increased refractoriness with each subsequent progression/relapse (Link et al. 2019;
Rivas-Delgado et al. 2019). Patients who have received ≥2 prior therapies have a particularly
poor prognosis, with a median PFS ranging from 1-1.1 years for third-line patients decreasing to
0.5 years for sixth-line patients with a corresponding median OS of 4.8-8.8 years and 1.9 years,
respectively (Alperovich et al. 2016; Rivas-Delgado et al. 2019; Batlevi et al. 2020).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Using data from the US Surveillance,
Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER program), the age adjusted incidence rate based on 20152019 cases was 2.6 per 100000 men and women. Rates were higher in men (2.8) among all
races than among women (2.4). Among men, rates were highest in Non-Hispanic Whites (3.2),
followed by Hispanics (2.6), Non-Hispanic American/Indian Alaskan Natives (1.9), Non-Hispanic
Asian/Pacific Islanders (1.7), and Non-Hispanic Blacks (1.3). Among women, rates were also
highest among Non-Hispanic Whites (2.7), followed by Hispanics (2.4), Non-Hispanic
American/Indian Alaskan Natives (1.8), Non-Hispanic Asian/Pacific Islanders (1.4), and NonHispanic Blacks (1.2).

Analysis of Current Treatment Options

The Applicant’s Position:
Relapse to initial therapy for FL is almost inevitable. In the relapsed/refractory setting,
treatment decisions take into consideration efficacy and DOR of prior therapy, stage of disease
and tumor burden at relapse, the presence of symptoms, and the age and comorbidities of the
patient.
For patients who require second-line therapy, there is no treatment considered standard of
25
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
care, and options vary widely. Rituximab as a single agent, bendamustine, obinutuzumab in
combination with bendamustine followed by obinutuzumab monotherapy, lenalidomide plus a
rituximab-product, and the radioimmunotherapy 90Y-ibritumomab tiuxetan have all received
regular full approval for the treatment of R/R FL in the US.
In the third-line setting, certain treatment options approved for first- and/or second-line FL are
available treatment options. However, patients who have become refractory to anti-CD20directed therapy or alkylating agents will unlikely derive treatment benefit by re-using these
regimens. In later relapses, a non-chemotherapy approach is recommended. Agents under
accelerated approval specifically for patients with relapsed or refractory FL after at least two
prior therapies (and not considered to be available therapy because clinical benefit is yet to be
confirmed) are the PI3K inhibitors idelalisib, copanlisib, and duvelisib, and the histone
methyltransferase enhancer of zeste homolog 2 (EZH2) inhibitor tazemetostat, and the CD19directed chimeric antigen receptor-modified T-cell (CAR-T) therapy axicabtagene ciloleucel
(Yescarta). Umbralisib, a dual PI3K delta and casein kinase 1 epsilon inhibitor, has received
accelerated approval for the treatment of patients with R/R FL who have received at least three
prior lines of systemic therapy.
Specific details of the indications, and the efficacy and safety data supporting FDA-approved
drugs for patients with indications relevant to this FL application are listed in Table 1 below. Not
included in the table are chemotherapeutic agents (e.g., chlorambucil, cyclophosphamide,
vincristine, and doxorubicin) which are approved for use in the US to treat a broader spectrum
of neoplastic diseases including malignant lymphomas.
In addition to approved therapies, there are chemotherapy agents or combinations that are
used in the R/R setting, including some that are not FDA approved. Table 2 summarizes
suggested treatment regimens for second-line and subsequent therapy, and third-line and
subsequent therapy for the treatment of FL as described in the current National Comprehensive
Cancer Network Clinical Practice Guidelines in Oncology for B-cell Lymphomas (v4.2021).
Relapsed or refractory FL is a serious and life-threatening disease that remains incurable.
Relapse is almost inevitable, and a significant proportion of patients die from recurrent disease
or treatment-related toxicity. There is a high unmet need for patients with R/R FL who have
received ≥2 prior therapies, particularly for patients who are R/R to different classes of agents
and are left with limited treatment options that may have challenging safety profiles. New
treatment options that will significantly extend duration of remission and that can overcome
resistance to existing therapies, while providing acceptable safety and tolerability are needed.

The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position, but has provided additional
information. Per the Federal Register, the indications in follicular lymphoma for duvelisib,
idelalisib, and umbralisib were voluntarily withdrawn on 4/13/22, 5/26/22, and 5/31/22,
respectively. Copanlisib remains under accelerated approval for the treatment of adult patients
with relapsed follicular lymphoma who have received at least 2 prior systemic therapies.
Tisagenlecleucel, a C19-directed genetically modified autologous T cell immunotherapy, also
recently received accelerated approval on May 31, 2022, for adult patients with relapsed or
refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
Mosunetuzumab is a new molecular entity and is not currently marketed in any country.
The Biologics License Application (BLA) 761263 is being submitted for accelerated approval of
mosunetuzumab as a single agent for the indication of the treatment of adult patients with
relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma (FL) who have received at least two prior systemic
therapies.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical
Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
Clinical site inspections were requested for two clinical sites in the U.S. with the highest accrual
for Study GO29781, along with inspection of the Applicant, Genentech, Inc. The clinical sites
included 1) Site # 284510 – Washington University School of Medicine (Dr. Bartlett, MD; St.
Louis, MO), which enrolled 40 patients and 2) Site 284773 – City of Hope (Dr. Budde, MD;
Duarte, CA), which enrolled 66 patients. For both clinical sites inspections, no objectionable
conditions or practices were identified, and no actions were indicated. Therefore, based on the
clinical site inspections, the study data from the clinical sites were considered reliable.
Additionally, the Applicant was inspected concerning its oversight of GO29781. Based on the
inspection, no objectionable conditions or practices were identified, and no actions were
indicated. Thus, the Applicant’s oversight of the clinical trial and the quality of conducting the
study were determined to be adequate. The audited study data submitted to the Agency was
deemed acceptable in support of the BLA application, in compliance with Good Clinical Practice.

Product Quality
Mosunetuzumab is bispecific CD20-directed CD3 T-cell engager. It is a humanized monoclonal
anti-CD20xCD3 T-cell-dependent bispecific antibody of the immunoglobulin G1 (IgG1) isotype.
Mosunetuzumab-axgb is produced in Chinese Hamster Ovary (CHO) cells by recombinant DNA
technology. The approximate molecular weight is 146 kDa.
Mosunetuzumab-axgb injection is a sterile, preservative-free, colorless solution for intravenous
use.
Each single-dose vial contains a 1 mL solution of mosunetuzumab-axgb (1 mg), acetic acid
(0.4 mg), histidine (1.6 mg), methionine (1.5 mg), polysorbate 20 (0.6 mg), sucrose (82.1 mg),
and Water for Injection, USP. The pH is 5.8.
Each single-dose vial contains a 30 mL solution of mosunetuzumab-axgb (30 mg), acetic acid
(12.8 mg), histidine (46.6 mg), methionine (44.8 mg), polysorbate 20 (18 mg), sucrose
(2,462.4 mg), and Water for Injection, USP. The pH is 5.8.
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of mosunetuzumab-axgb
manufactured by Genentech, Inc. for human use under the conditions specified in the package
insert. The data submitted support the conclusion that the manufacture of mosunetuzumabaxgb is well-controlled and leads to a product that is pure and potent.

The Applicant’s Position:
(b) (4)
In huCD20/CD3 transgenic mice, after a single IV dose, mosunetuzumab and

leading to an eventual reduction of target-mediated CL.

prothrombin time and activated partial thromboplastin time at 1 mg/kg. Changes in clinical

decreased cholesterol and albumin (with associated decreases in total protein, albumin to
globulin ratio and calcium) at ≥ 0.1 mg/kg, and increased urea nitrogen and creatinine, and

BTCT4465A-related microscopic findings were observed in tissues including the lymphoid
system (spleen, thymus, lymph nodes and/or gut associated lymphoid tissue [GALT]), liver,
heart, and central nervous system (CNS) at ≥ 0.01 mg/kg IV and 1 mg/kg SC. In the spleen,
increased lymphocyte cellularity within the periarteriolar sheaths (PALS) and increased
phagocytic cells within the red pulp correlated with increased spleen weights. In the thymus,
decreased lymphocyte cellularity was noted that correlated with decreased thymus weights. In
GALT, decreased number and size of follicular germinal centers, and decreased number and size
of lymphoid follicles in the lymph nodes were observed at 1 mg/kg IV and SC. Findings in the
liver included with hypertrophy/hyperplasia of sinusoidal Kupffer cells at ≥ 0.1 mg/kg IV. These
findings were noted during the recovery necropsies with lower incidence on Day 22 in the
spleen, lymph nodes and liver and limited to 0.1 and 1 mg/kg (IV and SC), and on Day 57 in the
spleen and lymph nodes at 1 mg/kg IV. In CNS tissues, perivascular (generally) infiltrates of
eosinophils with associated microgliosis were observed in the brain and/or spinal cord and/or
spinal nerve roots at 1 mg/kg IV and SC. These changes were not associated with clinical
observations. No CNS findings were noted on Days 22 or 57.
BTCT4465A induced pharmacodynamic effects of rapid and sustained B-cell depletion in blood
and lymphoid tissues, increases in serum BAFF levels, dose-dependent reductions in T
lymphocyte populations (total, T-helper, and T-cytotoxic) and NK cells, T-cell activation
associated T-cell redistribution (total, Th and Tc lymphocytes), and cytokine/chemokine
increases (IL-1RA, IL-2, IL-5, IL-6, IL-13, IL-17, G-CSF, TNF-α, and IFN-γ) in all groups (≥ 0.01
mg/kg) at the 2 hours post-dose time point. These changes trended toward recovery by Day 8
through Day 57.
Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
In accordance with the current ICH guidance (ICH S6(R1) 2011), genotoxicity studies have not
been conducted with mosunetuzumab because the potential of biotechnology products, in
particular high molecular weight monoclonal antibodies, to elicit DNA damage in somatic or
germ cells via electrophilic reactions is unlikely.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA concurs with the Applicant.

Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted with mosunetuzumab and are not planned for
the intended oncology indication, as per Guidance for Industry S6R1.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA concurs with the Applicant.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Male and female reproductive assessments were conducted as a part of the 26-week chronic
toxicity study in sexually mature cynomolgus monkeys (Study 16-1815). Evaluation included
organ weights, and macroscopic and microscopic evaluation of male and female reproductive
organs (including qualitative spermatogenesis progression for males and general phases of
menstrual cycles for females). There were no mosunetuzumab-related effects identified in the
male or female reproductive assessment.
A weight of evidence (WOE)-based risk assessment for potential developmental effects was
conducted. Based on low placental transfer of antibodies during the first trimester, the
mechanism of action and available data of mosunetuzumab, and the data from other approved
anti-CD20 antibodies developed by the Applicant (e.g., rituximab [Rituxan USPI],
obinutuzumab [Gazyva USPI], ocrelizumab [Ocrevus USPI]), the risk for teratogenicity is low.
Studies with mosunetuzumab in non-pregnant animals have demonstrated that prolonged
B-cell depletion in the dam can lead to increased risk of opportunistic infection, which may
result in fetal loss, as shown with ocrelizumab [Ocrevus USPI]). Transient CRS associated with
mosunetuzumab administration may also be harmful to pregnancy.
A briefing book, including the WOE-based risk assessment and a request to waive a non-human
primate embryo-fetal and prenatal/postnatal development study, was submitted to the FDA
(dated 3 April 2018; Reference ID: 4277993). In a written communication from the FDA dated
14 June 2018, the Agency agreed to the waiver request. During the End-of-Phase-I Type B
meeting on 18 March, 2020, the Agency recommended to remove the list of products for which
the Sponsor does not have the right-to-reference, and considered other components used in
the WOE-based risk assessment reasonable for submission. The updated risk assessment
document is included in Module 4 (Assessment of Developmental Effects).

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
The FDA’s Assessment:
To address the potential effects of mosunetuzumab on embryo-fetal development, the
Applicant submitted an assessment for reproductive and developmental risk using a weight-ofevidence (WOE) approach and with the recommendation in the ICH S6(R1) guideline. The
relevant information is summarized below and supports that the main reproductive risk for
mosunetuzumab is B-cell depletion in neonates and includes information from other products
that the Applicant (Genentech, Inc.) owns or have the right of reference to.
The WOE risk assessment includes the following information; references cited were provided by
the Applicant:
1. Maternal and/or neonatal toxicities have been observed with the FDA approved antiCD20 antibodies, including rituximab, obinutuzumab and ocrelizumab.
a. Obinutuzumab crosses the blood-placental barrier and it is secreted in the milk
of pregnant monkeys. There was complete depletion of B lymphocytes in infants,
and higher prenatal loss in treatment groups compared to controls but within
the range of historical control data.
2. Based on its mechanism of action and pharmacodynamic effects seen in animals,
mosunetuzumab may cause depletion of B-cells and impair the immune response after
birth due to fetal exposure to mosunetuzumab. Impaired immune response may likely
be associated with opportunistic infections. Secondary infections due to B-cell depletion
and associated mortality/early euthanasia were observed in cynomolgus monkeys
following prolonged treatment with mosunetuzumab in the 26-week toxicity study.
3. Mosunetuzumab-related toxicities including vomiting, diarrhea, hypoactivity/hunched
posture, hypotension, tachycardia, fever, acute phase protein reactions, leukocyte
margination, in cynomolgus monkeys may pose a risk to the maintenance of pregnancy.
Other Toxicology Studies
The Applicant’s Position:
An in vitro assay was performed in human PBMCs to assess cytokine release and T cell
activation induced by mosunetuzumab. The results (EC20 values) were used to inform the
starting dose in the first in human study. Additionally, the same assay was used to evaluate the
(b) (4)
potential impact of
a product-related variant that may be formed at low
levels in the production of mosunetuzumab, on the activities of mosunetuzumab. The results
(b) (4)
were used to support the proposed control strategy
for the
commercial material. A tissue cross-reactivity study could not be conducted due to poor
analytical sensitivity and specificity of mosunetuzumab as an IHC reagent.

The primary efficacy and safety evidence supporting the proposed dosage regimen results from
the pivotal B11 expansion cohort in Study GO29781, an ongoing first-in-human, multicenter,
open-label, Phase I/II dose escalation and expansion study demonstrating an objective
response rate (ORR) of 80% and a complete response rate (CR) of 60%, as well as an overall
acceptable safety profile.
The key review issues focused on the evaluation of dose selection, evaluation of dosage
recommendations for restarting therapy after dose delay, and drug-drug interaction (DDI) due
to cytokine release.
Recommendations
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information submitted in BLA761263. This
BLA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific
recommendations/comments are summarized below in Table 10.
Table 10: Key Clinical Pharmacology Review Issues and Recommendations by FDA
Review Issue

Recommendations and Comments

Pivotal and Supportive
evidence of effectiveness

The primary evidence of effectiveness comes from the pivotal
B11 FL RP2D cohort in study GO29781. The proposed dosage
regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) is supported by an ORR of 80% and CR
of 60% in patients with relapsed or refractory FL.

General dosing
instructions

The proposed dosage regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) is selected with
the following rationale:
The step-up doses (1 mg and 2 mg) are clinically active
doses that help mitigate CRS risk in later cycles.
• No exposure response trends were observed for safety
endpoints except for Grade ≥2 CRS, at the proposed dose
of 1/2/60/30 mg, the observed Grade ≥2 CRS and the
Exposure-Response model predicted that the probability
for Grade ≥2 CRS over the first two cycles is relatively low.
• There were positive exposure response relationships for

Majority of CR (50 out of 54 cases) were achieved within 8

Patients who received PR or SD after 8 cycles of treatment
benefited (deepened response from PR to CR, or received

Dosage recommendations
for restarting therapy after
dose delay

The proposed dosage recommendation for restarting therapy
after dose delay is to mitigate CRS risk and is supported by:
• Observed clinical data showing that CRS was mitigated.
• PopPK simulations showing that mosunetuzumab
concentrations were maintained approximately at or above
concentration levels achieved with 2 mg prior to
administration of the 60 mg loading dose, consistent with
the proposed dosing regimen (1/2/60/30 mg) and the
associated CRS mitigation strategy.

Dosing in patient
subgroups (intrinsic and
extrinsic factors)

No dosage adjustment is needed in patient subgroups. The
effect of severe renal impairment to end‐stage renal disease
(CLcr < 30 mL/min) and moderate to severe hepatic impairment
(total bilirubin > 3 to 10x ULN, any AST) is unknown.

Drug-drug interactions

Mosunetuzumab administration resulted in transient release of
cytokines, which may suppress CYP450 enzymes and cause
drug-drug interactions. Increased exposure of CYP450
substrates is more likely to occur after the first dose of
mosunetuzumab on Cycle 1 Day 1 and up to 14 days after the
second loading dose on Cycle 2 Day 1 and during and after CRS.
Monitor for toxicity or concentrations of drugs that are CYP450
substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to
serious adverse reactions. Adjust the dose of the concomitant
drug as needed.

Labeling

Overall, the proposed labeling recommendations are
acceptable upon the Applicant’s agreement with the FDA
recommended revisions to the labeling.

Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics

The evaluation of the clinical pharmacology of mosunetuzumab to support this BLA application
was based on clinical data from the IV monotherapy cohorts of Study GO29781, including the
fixed dosing group (Group A; 0.05-2.8 mg IV q3w) and the Cycle 1 step-up dosing group

individual-level covariates for mosunetuzumab in patients with NHL, are provided.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

The intended registration dose and schedule of mosunetuzumab is administered using the
Cycle 1 step-up regimen (i.e., 1 mg and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 8, respectively, as the “step
doses”, followed by two “loading doses” of 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1, and
then maintained at the “base dose” of 30 mg for the remaining Cycles 3−8; treatment continues
for 17 cycles [approximately 1 year] if CR is not achieved by Cycle 8), hereafter referred to as
1/2/60/30 mg.
A schematic of the intended registration dose and schedule is shown in Figure 9.

The rationale for the intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg is summarized
below:
•

•

•

“Step Doses” of 1 mg and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 8 are pharmacodynamically and
clinically active and well tolerated doses shown to mitigate the maximum receptor
occupancy (ROmax)-dependency of the acute CRS.
“Loading Doses” of 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1 are predicted to achieve
maximal clinical response (i.e., CRR and ORR) by exposure-response analyses in the
proposed patient population.
“Base Doses” of 30 mg on Day 1 in each cycle in Cycles 3 and beyond are expected to
achieve maximal and durable clinical response following the initial tumor debulking
after the first 2 cycles based on the totality of data.

The model–predictions based on integrated ER analyses of efficacy and safety for Study
GO29781 including patients with R/R FL indicate a broad therapeutic window of
mosunetuzumab with minimal/low exposure-dependency for the overall safety risk using the
Cycle 1 step-up dosing regimen. The integrated ER analyses, combined with the observed
clinical efficacy and safety data, provide strong support for the RP2D/intended registration dose
and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg to achieve a favorable clinical benefit-risk profile in patients with
R/R FL, with high clinical response rate (model-predicted probability for CRR and ORR of
approximately 63% and 79%, respectively) and low (<12%) probability of a patient experiencing

6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data:
The mosunetuzumab population PK (PopPK) analysis dataset included data from
439 PK-evaluable patients from Study GO29781 who received mosunetuzumab by IV
administration. Body weight, tumor burden, albumin, previous anti-CD20 drug concentrations,
and sex were found to be statistically significant covariates on mosunetuzumab PK with
marginal impact on drug exposure. None of these covariates resulted in more than 20% change
in mosunetuzumab exposure (area under the concentration–time curve from time 0 to 42 days,
AUC0-42), when evaluated at extreme values compared to the typical patients. Neither patient
age, renal impairment status, hepatic impairment status, drug material used, histology (i.e., FL
vs. DLBCL vs. other), nor race (Asian vs. non-Asian) were identified as covariates influencing
mosunetuzumab PK. Effects of covariates of special interest (body weight, age, gender, race ,
hepatic function and renal function, NHL histology) on mosunetuzumab PK were summarized
below:
Body Weight
The body weight effect on mosunetuzumab exposure was relatively small compared to the
overall inter-individual variability of the population and was not clinically relevant. There does
not appear to be an impact of body size on efficacy of mosunetuzumab.
Age
Age had no statistically significant impact on the mosunetuzumab PK parameters. The age
effect on mosunetuzumab exposure was relatively small compared to the overall interindividual variability of the population and was not clinically relevant. There were no
differences in safety and efficacy of mosunetuzumab observed by age subgroup.
Gender
The mosunetuzumab AUC0-42 for a typical female patient (weighing 78 kg) is approximately 6%
higher than a typical male patient and not considered clinically meaningful. There were no
differences in safety and efficacy of mosunetuzumab observed by gender subgroup.
Race
Race (Asian vs. non-Asian) had no statistically significant impact on the mosunetuzumab PK
72
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
parameters. The race effect on mosunetuzumab exposure was relatively small compared to the
overall inter-individual variability of the population and was not clinically relevant. There were
no differences in efficacy of mosunetuzumab observed by race subgroup.
Hepatic Impairment
None of the lab values tested (i.e., AST, ALT, total bilirubin) was a statistically significant
covariate on mosunetuzumab PK. Hepatic function category had no apparent effect on model
predicted mosunetuzumab PK exposure. The limited data indicated that mosunetuzumab
exposures in patients with mild hepatic impairment were not meaningfully different compared
to those with normal hepatic function. The number of patients with moderate hepatic
impairment is limited (n=2) and no patients with severe hepatic impairment have been studied.
No dose recommendation can be made for mosunetuzumab in patients with moderate or
severe hepatic impairment.
Renal Impairment
CrCL was not a statistically significant covariate on mosunetuzumab PK. Renal function category
had no apparent effect on model predicted mosunetuzumab PK exposure. However, the
number of patients with severe renal impairment is limited (n=1) and no patients on dialysis
have been studied, and a recommended dose for mosunetuzumab has not been determined for
these patients.
NHL histology
NHL histology (DLBCL, FL, and others) had no statistically significant impact on the
mosunetuzumab PK parameters. Mosunetuzumab PK exposure (AUC0-42) is comparable across
NHL histology groups.
The Applicant’s Position:
Given the totality of the data based on results from the mosunetuzumab PopPK analysis,
together with subgroup analysis for the efficacy and safety, no clinically meaningful covariate
was identified that warrants dose adjustment for mosunetuzumab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The Applicant’s population PK analysis for
mosunetuzumab is considered acceptable for the purpose of supporting analyses objectives.
Body weight (37.1 to 163 kg), tumor burden (96.0 to 70900 mm2), albumin (19.0 to 480 g/L),
baseline residual anti-CD20 drug concentrations, and sex (Female [n = 155, 35.3%] and Male [n
= 284, 64.7%]) were found to be statistically significant covariates on mosunetuzumab
pharmacokinetics with marginal impact on drug exposure that is not clinically meaningful
(Figure 15).

The proposed dosage recommendations are based on the same instructions included in the

treatment occurs predominantly during the first cycle of treatment, the risk may re-emerge
76
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
when a patient is off treatment for a prolonged period of time. Therefore, to mitigate an
increase of CRS risk in patients with dose delay, a repeat step-up dosing is recommended. In
addition, in all scenarios outlined above, the recommendation ensures that the patient receives
both 60 mg loading doses followed by the continued 30 mg Q3W doses in subsequent cycles to
maintain cumulative on-study exposure similar to that achieved with the nominal proposed
dosing regimen of 1/2/60/30 mg, given the established positive benefit-risk profile at this
dosage and the positive E-R relationship for efficacy.
The above recommendations are supported by:
• Observed clinical data showing that among the 14 patients who met the above dose
delay scenarios, 13 out of 14 followed the recommendations and did not experience CRS
after treatment resumption. One patient experienced CRS after treatment resumption,
however, this patient was enrolled before the proposed recommendations became
effective following a protocol amendment and did not follow the above
recommendations.
• Supportive population PK simulations showed that when the dose delay was longer,
mosunetuzumab concentration would fall below the drug levels obtained with the step
up doses (1 mg and 2 mg) on Cycle 1 Day 1 and Day 8, putting the patient at increased
risk of CRS due to potential resensitization to mosunetuzumab treatment; repeat stepup doses as recommended above will maintain mosunetuzumab concentrations at or
above concentration levels achieved with 2 mg prior to administration of the higher 60
mg loading dose and 30 mg base dose, consistent with the proposed dosing regimen
(1/2/60/30 mg) and the associated CRS mitigation strategy. Population PK Model

Figure 19, Figure 20, Figure 21 and Figure 22 below.

variability, and individual-level covariates for mosunetuzumab in patients with NHL are
summarized below.
Key findings:
• Non-compartmental analyses (NCA) indicate that mosunetuzumab serum concentration
reaches the maximal level (maximum drug concentration in serum [Cmax]) at the end of
infusion and declines in a bi-exponential fashion, with an apparent half-life (t1/2) of
approximately 3 to 10 days. The terminal half-life estimate was 16.1 days at steady state
based on population pharmacokinetics (popPK) model simulations.
• The serum concentration-time data was well-described by a popPK model with two
compartment and time-dependent clearance (CL), which was parameterized as an initial
baseline clearance (CLbase ~1.08 L/day) which transitions over time to a steady state
clearance (CLss ~0.584 L/day) with a transition half-life (HLtrans) of 16.3 days. The CL values
are higher than the typical range of clearance of a typical IgG1 antibody, suggesting
potential impact of target-mediated drug disposition (TMDD).
• The central volume of distribution (V1) was 5.49 L and peripheral volume of distribution
(V2) was 6.17 L. The central volume of distribution of 5.49 L is larger than plasma volume,
suggesting the potential impact of binding to immediately accessible targets following IV
administration of mosunetuzumab.
• Mosunetuzumab exhibits linear and dose-proportional PK in the dose range studied (0.2 to
60 mg) and in the clinically active dose range (≥1.2 mg).
• No clinically meaningful baseline covariates were found for mosunetuzumab
pharmacokinetics requiring dose adjustments.
o Moderate to high inter-individual variability (IIV) was observed for mosunetuzumab
key PK parameters (range from 18% to 86%).
o Significant baseline covariate effects include body weight on CLss, V1 and V2,
albumin on CLbase and V1, sex on CLss and V1, tumor burden on CLss, and baseline
anti-CD20 (aCD20) drug concentrations on CLbase. However, none of these covariates
resulted in more than 20% change in mosunetuzumab pharmacokinetics exposure
(area under the concentration–time curve from time 0 to 42 days, AUC0-42), when
evaluated at extreme values compared to the typical patient.
Pharmacokinetics in Special Populations
See 6.2.2.2.
Material Comparability
PopPK modeling indicate similar pharmacokinetics between the v0.1 (used in dose escalation
cohorts) and v0.2 materials (used in the dose escalation and expansion cohorts, including
pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort).
Exposure-Response Relationships
83
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
The exposure-response (ER) relationships for efficacy were characterized based on investigator
(INV)-assessed clinical objective response data from 159 patients with R/R FL who had received
more than two prior therapies and treated with IV administration of mosunetuzumab in
Study GO29781. The ER relationships for safety were assessed based on 439 PK–evaluable
patients with R/R NHL receiving IV administration of mosunetuzumab from Study GO29781. Key
findings are summarized below.
ER analyses for biomarker:
•

The ER analyses for pharmacodynamic biomarker support evidence of downstream
pharmacologic activity and indicate on-study change in IFN−γ to be associated with
increasing maximal receptor occupancy (ROmax) following Cycle 1 Day 1 dose (“1st Dose”).
However, the ER relationships following the subsequent Cycle 1 mosunetuzumab doses
remained relatively flat. The shift (i.e., reduction) in ER relationship is consistent with the
mitigation of acute cytokine release from T cells upon repeat dosing of mosunetuzumab.
The Cycle-1 step doses of 1 mg and 2 mg are pharmacodynamically active and CRSmitigating. The ER and peripheral IFN-γ data support the use of Cycle 1 step-up dosing
regimen to mitigate acute cytokine-driven toxicity of mosunetuzumab.

ER analyses for efficacy:
• The ER analyses indicate that clinical responses (complete response rate [CRR] and objective
response rate [ORR]) increase with increasing mosunetuzumab exposure (AUC0-42) and
approach a plateau estimated by the model as the maximal clinical responses of 68.6 % and
81.4% for CRR and ORR, respectively.
• ER analysis showed a trend of longer median PFS with increased mosunetuzumab exposure
(AUC0-42).
• The duration of response (DOR) (for OR and CR) was similar at the 1/2/60/30 mg dose
compared to the 1/2/13.5 mg dose. ER analysis showed a trend of longer DOR (for OR and
CR) with increasing exposure (AUC0-42).
ER analyses for safety:
• CRS following step-up dosing of mosunetuzumab is primarily low grade and the occurrence
is mostly confined to Cycle 1. CRS events are less frequent at Cycle 2 and onwards.
• ER analyses indicate a positive E-R relationship where the occurrence of Grade ≥2 CRS
increases modestly with higher maximal receptor occupancy (ROmax) values. ER analyses by
dosing groups (Group A – fixed dosing regimen; Group B: Cycle-1 step-up dosing regimen)
indicate that the exposure-dependent increase in Grade ≥2 CRS observed at the lower dose
range (0.05 to 2.8 mg) tested in Group A using the fixed dosing regimen was mitigated in
Group B by Cycle 1 step-up regimen.
• CRS ER analyses (stratified by mosunetuzumab Cycle 1 Day 1 dose, Day 8 dose, and Day 15
dose) indicated higher ROmax values to be associated with modest increasing rates of
84
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}

•

•

Grade ≥2 CRS upon the initial doses in Cycle 1. However, E-R trends upon weekly repeat
dosing in Cycle 1 suggest a pattern of similar/ decreased CRS risk at comparatively higher
exposures in subsequent doses, thus supporting the step-up regimen approach as an
effective CRS mitigation strategy.
The Cycle 1 step-up dosing schedule mitigated the acute CRS risks and resulted in a
favorable safety profile with predicted low frequency of Grade ≥2 CRS (less than 12%) at the
RP2D/intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg.
Dosing of mosunetuzumab following the step-up dosing regimen does not lead to exposuredependent increases for Grade ≥3 neutropenia, Grade ≥3 infections or infestations, any
Grade ≥3 adverse events (AEs), or any Grade ≥1 AEs. Higher mosunetuzumab exposure does
not lead to an earlier onset of AEs.

Immunogenicity
The potential of mosunetuzumab to induce an immune response is being assessed in the
ongoing clinical Study GO29781. All patients in the study are being monitored as required at
regular intervals for the development of anti-mosunetuzumab antibodies.
As of the ADA data cutoff date of 4 December 2020, there was no ADA detected in 418 ADA
evaluable patients who received mosunetuzumab single agent IV treatments in Study GO29781.
The observation of no ADA based on the available data is consistent with the expected
mechanism of action of B cell killing.
The Applicant’s Position:
Collectively, the PK and E-R analyses support intended registration dose and schedule of
1/2/60/30 mg in patients with R/R FL and support a positive benefit-risk profile.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Mosunetuzumab PK properties, E-R relationships
and immunogenicity assessment have been well characterized and support the proposed dose
and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg in patients with R/R FL.
• PK: see Section 6.2.1 and 6.2.2.2.
• E-R for Efficacy:
― The E-R efficacy analyses indicated that clinical responses (CR ,ORR) increased with
increasing mosunetuzumab exposure (AUC0–42) and approached a plateau at the
proposed dosage of 1/2/60/30 mg. The covariate analysis indicated that patients
with larger tumors or with high predose anti-CD20 antibody concentrations are
predicted to have a lower probability to attain CR, though still expected to be higher
than 50% for CR.
― The E-R efficacy analyses indicated a longer median PFS with increased
mosunetuzumab exposure (AUC0–42), and a longer median PFS at the 1/2/60/30 mg
dose (B11 cohort, RP2D) compared to the 1/2/13.5 mg dose (B7 cohort).

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of
effectiveness?

Data:
Exposure-response for clinical CRR and ORR were characterized in 159 patients with R/R FL
across a 300-fold dose range (i.e., 0.2 to 60 mg) and across 600-fold of AUC0–42 values
(0.400-251 day•µg/mL), to properly inform the characterization of the ER relationships. Clinical
response increases in an exposure-dependent manner and plateaus at the AUC0–42 values of
approximately 92.7 and 38.4 day*µg/mL corresponding to the estimated E90 for CRR and ORR,
respectively. The intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg is predicted to
achieve PK exposures at the plateau of the E-R curves for CRR and ORR and thus maximizes the
therapeutic potential in the patient population. The proposed 1/2/60/30 mg leads to higher
predicted CRR and ORR compared to the lower dose of 1/2/13.5 mg, which is consistent with
the clinical observations (INV-assessed: CRR/ORR = 60%/77.8% and 47.8%/63.0% for B11 and
B7 cohorts, respectively; IRF-assessed: CRR/ORR=60%/80% and 45.7%/67.4% for B11 and B7
cohorts, respectively).
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on totality of data, the Applicant believes the mosunetuzumab clinical pharmacology
program together with the efficacy results from Study GO29781 provides sufficient evidence of

6.3.2.4 Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is
the appropriate management strategy?
87
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
Mosunetuzumab is administered intravenously (bioavailability=1.0) and no food effect would
be expected with parenteral administration.
Physiologically-based pharmacokinetics (PBPK) modeling and simulations based on IL-6 and
CYP3A4 interaction indicated a low risk of cytokine-mediated drug-drug interaction (DDI)
potential of mosunetuzumab on cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzyme expression or activity. The
predicted area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) and maximum concentration (Cmax)
ratios (with mosunetuzumab co-administration/without mosunetuzumab co-administration) for
midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A substrate was 1.37 and 1.17, respectively. No dose adjustment is
recommended for mosunetuzumab when co-dosing mosunetuzumab with small molecule drugs
which are CYP3A substrates.
The potential impact of baseline obinutuzumab (G) or rituximab (R) concentration on the PK
and clinical response of mosunetuzumab was evaluated in the population PK and exposureresponse (E-R) models based on study GO29781 including patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior
therapies. Baseline G or R concentration was determined not to be a clinically meaningful
covariate on PK and response. The lack of clinically meaningful changes in the clinical response
of mosunetuzumab in the presence of residual R or G is consistent with expectations, given the
indolent nature of the disease which allows for a broader time window for response. No dose
adjustment is recommended for mosunetuzumab based on prior anti-CD20 therapies.
The Applicant’s Position:
There was no evidence for clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions for
mosunetuzumab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Mosunetuzumab can cause transient release of cytokines that may potentially suppress CYP450
enzymes. Based on evaluation of the totality of data, FDA considers the risk of drug interactions
to be low. FDA’s conclusion is based on the following considerations:
• The elevations of cytokines observed after mosunetuzumab doses are transient in
nature and attenuate with repeat dosing.
• The highest drug‐drug interaction risk is expected to occur from initiation of
mosunetuzumab step-up dosing schedule up to 14 days after the second loading dose
(60 mg) on Cycle 2 Day 1, and during and after CRS.
• Label recommends monitoring for toxicity or concentrations of drugs that are CYP450
substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions.
See Section 20.4.7 for detailed assessment of the PBPK modeling and simulations to support
the above conclusions.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
GO29781
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
The clinical efficacy and safety results supporting this application are from a single ongoing
multicenter, open-label, Phase I/II dose-escalation and dose-expansion study GO29781 of
mosunetuzumab administered as a single agent (and in combination with atezolizumab) in
patients with R/R hematologic malignancies expected to express CD20, including B-cell NHL and
CLL.
The initial patients enrolled were treated with escalating doses of single-agent mosunetuzumab
administered IV on Day 1 of each 3-weekly (q3w) cycle (Group A fixed dosing schedule).
Subsequent protocol amendments were implemented to expand the study to evaluate a
different IV dosing schedule (Group B Cycle 1 step-up dosing), include a dose expansion phase
to evaluate mosunetuzumab at a recommended expansion dose in specific B-cell NHL
histologies, and introduce efficacy as a co-primary objective. Further dose escalation and
expansion cohorts were also introduced to evaluate mosunetuzumab IV in combination with
atezolizumab (Group E), and mosunetuzumab administered as a single agent by SC injection
(Groups D and F). The study was re-classified from Phase I/Ib to Phase I/II to reflect the overall
size of the study, and the study objective to investigate the safety, efficacy, and PK/PD of the
recommended Phase II dose (RP2D) in expansion cohorts with defined NHL histologies (protocol
v12, dated 23 February 2021).
The GO29781 study enrolled adult patients with a history of a hematologic malignancy that is
expected to express the CD20 antigen, including FL, MZL, DLBCL, PMBCL, transformed indolent
NHL, MCL, Richter's transformation, and CLL/SLL. Confirmation of CD20 expression was not
required during eligibility screening prior to enrollment and was evaluated retrospectively
based on data from a central lab. Patients included in the pivotal R/R FL expansion cohort had
to have histologically-documented Grade 1-3a FL, measurable disease, must have relapsed after
or failed to respond to at least two prior lines of systemic therapy and must have received prior
treatment of an anti-CD20 directed therapy and an alkylating agent.
Results to support this application are from Groups A and B only, which evaluated
mosunetuzumab as a single agent administered by IV infusion.
91
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
After completion of the first eight dose escalation cohorts, further enrolment into Group A was
stopped beyond 2.8 mg (the highest assessed dose in Group A) to prioritize assessment of other
dosing schedules and routes of mosunetuzumab administration. The Group B Cycle 1 step-up
dosing schedule evaluated single-agent mosunetuzumab administered IV as incremental doses
on C1D1, C1D8, and C1D15 dose levels followed by q3w dosing on Day 1 of subsequent cycles.
The dose escalation evaluated 11 dose levels (ranging from 0.4/1/2.8 mg to 1/2/60 mg
[B1-B11]), according to a standard 3+3 design. The Group B dose-expansion stage was designed
to obtain additional safety, tolerability, PK, and clinical efficacy data with single agent
mosunetuzumab administered IV by Cycle 1 step-up dosing in one or more dose expansion
cohorts at dose levels at or below the MTD (which was not reached). The selected RP2D, which
is also the intended registration dose, and schedule was 1/2/60/30 mg (i.e., 1 mg on C1D1,
2 mg on C1D8, 60 mg on C1D15 and C2D1, and 30 mg for C3D1 and subsequent q3w cycles),
and was chosen based on cumulative data and additional analyses on exposure-efficacy and
exposure-safety relationships (see 6.2.2.1). The RP2D and schedule was evaluated in four
indication-specific expansion cohorts; R/R FL, R/R DLBCL/trFL, R/R MCL, and R/R Richter’s
transformation.
Patients who achieved a CR after 8 cycles received a maximum of 8 cycles, and patients who
achieved a PR or maintained stable disease after 8 cycles could receive a maximum of 17 cycles.
Patients who achieved a CR with initial single agent mosunetuzumab treatment and
subsequently relapsed (PD) could receive re-treatment with mosunetuzumab for at least
8 additional cycles. Patients were withdrawn from treatment due to disease progression or
relapse, unacceptable toxicity, use of NALT, patient or investigator decision or pregnancy.
Mosunetuzumab dose could be interrupted (delayed) for specific treatment emergent AEs (e.g.
DLTs, Grade 3 or serious AE) for up to 2 weeks in order to recover from the toxicity. The
mosunetuzumab dose could be continued with or without dose reduction. Patients who
completed or discontinued initial study treatment without disease progression continued to be
monitored, including regularly scheduled tumor assessments in post-initial treatment followup. Patients who discontinued treatment due to disease progression or experienced disease
progression in post-initial treatment follow-up could be followed for survival and subsequent
anti-cancer therapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study design. Study GO29781 is an openlabel, multicenter, multi-cohort study that included a cohort of patients with relapsed or
refractory follicular lymphoma who have received at least two prior therapies, including an
anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an alkylating agent. Patients were treated with
mosunetuzumab at the recommended dose and schedule (1/2/60/30 mg).
Study Endpoints
92
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary efficacy endpoint of IRF-assessed CR rate according to standard NHL response
criteria (Cheson et al. 2007) was introduced when efficacy became a co-primary objective of the
study at the time the study expanded to become a Phase Ib design (Protocol v4, dated
14 June 2017). This amendment was prospectively planned and implemented before any
patient had been enrolled into the Group B histology-specific expansion cohorts.
CR rate was chosen as the primary endpoint as it is clinically relevant, directly attributable to
drug effect and not the natural history of the disease, is based on objective and quantitative
assessments, and appropriate for a single arm study. Several lines of evidence indicate that CR
rate is reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit in patients with FL to support accelerated
approval. The quality (depth) of response is associated with better outcomes both in the firstline (Shi et al. 2017; Trotman et al. 2018) and R/R setting (Liu et al. 2020) and two metaanalyses of large databases of published trial data suggest that CR rate may be a potential
surrogate for PFS in FL (Zhu et al. 2017; Mangal et al. 2018). Additionally, the NCI Lymphoma
Clinical Trials Planning Meeting Follicular Subcommittee recommended in trials for patients
with high-risk R/R FL, the primary endpoints be PET-negative CR at the end of therapy and/or
continuous CR at 30 months (Maddocks et al. 2017) (see Section 1.7.3. of the Clinical Overview,
Module 2.5 for details).
In order to minimize the potential for investigator bias and ensure objectivity in interpretation
of results by the investigator in this open-label study, the primary endpoint was based on tumor
assessments reviewed independently by an IRF.
As GO29781 is a single arm study, the primary endpoint of IRF-assessed CR rate achieved by
patients in the pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort was compared statistically (exact binomial test with
(b)
two-sided alpha level of 5%) with an historical control CR rate assumed to be (4) % for the R/R FL
≥2 prior therapies population based on the CR rate reported for copanlisib in the CHRONOS-1
study (Dreyling et al. 2017).
Secondary efficacy endpoints evaluated in Study GO29781 were CR rate by INV, ORR, DOCR,
DOR, PFS (all by IRF and INV), OS and PRO measures. The analysis of these endpoints was
prospectively planned in the Statistical Analysis Plan (SAP; see below).
This primary analysis of efficacy was conducted when both of the following were met:
● At least approximately 6 months after the last patient in (LPI) in the B11 FL RP2D and
B11 DLBCL/trFL RP2D expansion cohorts, whichever was later.
• Efficacy evaluable population included approximately 80 patients in this cohort for each
histology.

Protocol Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The initial GO29781 study protocol, dated 23 April 2015 was amended 11 times up to the
version current at the time of the CCOD for the updated analysis supporting the application
(v12, dated 11 March 2021). The key amendments relevant to the evaluation of efficacy and
safety in cohorts of patient receiving mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy were as follows:
• Group B to evaluate Cycle 1 step-up doing to minimize potential toxicity with
mosunetuzumab IV was introduced (v2, 13 August 2016)
• Further enrolment into the dose escalation of Group A (q3w fixed dosing) was stopped
beyond 2.8 mg to prioritize assessment of other dosing schedules and routes of
administration (v4, 14 June 2017)
• The efficacy of mosunetuzumab using the Group B dosing schedule in patients with R/R FL
as measured by IRF-assessed CR rates was added as a co-primary objective of the study. In
addition, secondary objectives were expanded to includes PRO-based assessment of HRQoL
in the NHL expansion cohorts (v6, 27 November 2017)
• Several amendments to the protocol were made to incorporate additional risk monitoring
and mitigation plans based on evolving clinical data including additional guidance provided
for mitigation, management and reporting of CRS events, and neurologic events.
None of the modifications to the protocol were considered to have an impact on the integrity
of the trial or the interpretation of the efficacy or safety data.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of the key protocol amendments for Study
GO29781.

Patient Disposition
The Applicant’s Position:
Between 15 September 2015 (first-patient enrolled) and 27 August 2021 (the CCOD for the
updated analysis), 447 patients were enrolled into Groups A (dose escalation; n=33) and B
(dose escalation and expansion; n=414) to receive mosunetuzumab as a single agent by IV
infusion. Patients were enrolled at 40 sites in 7 countries (Australia, Canada, Germany, S. Korea,
Spain, United Kingdom, and US).
In Group B, 218 patients received the RP2D/intended registration dose and schedule (the
primary safety population), including 90 patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies (primary
efficacy population).
As of the CCOD, none of the patients in the pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort continued to receive
initial treatment, 41 patients were in post-initial treatment follow-up (without disease
progression), 35 patients were in survival follow-up (including one patient who received retreatment), 2 patients were active on first re-treatment, and 12 patients had discontinued from
the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. As shown in Table 18, no patients in the pivotal
B11 FL RP2D cohort and two in the B11 RP2D cohort were still on active treatment by the data
cutoff. The major reason for treatment discontinuation was progressive disease, which
accounted for 28% and 50% of patients in the pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort and B11 RP2D cohort,
respectively.

96
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

•
•

Eleven patients had major deviations categorized as incomplete/missing/out-of-window
tumor-related assessments as a bone marrow biopsy was not performed at screening
and/or within 42 days of the radiographic complete response to confirm a CR. In each case,
the CR was downgraded to a PR at that tumor assessment visit, and the efficacy endpoints
of CR rate and duration of CR reflect this conservative assessment.
One patient in the B11 FL RP2D cohort with negative serologic test results for acute or
chronic hepatitis B (HBV) infection at screening received their first dose of mosunetuzumab
prior to a positive hepatitis B virus (HBV) PCR result on the same day. Per protocol, patients
with known active infections were to be excluded from the study. HBV PCR negativity was
achieved and maintained during mosunetuzumab treatment with concomitant antiviral
treatment and the patient achieved PR as their best overall response.
One patient received 10 cycles of mosunetuzumab treatment despite achieving a CR after
8 cycles.
One patient had a delayed treatment cycle due to the COVID-19 pandemic after a CR was
achieved. Another patient had an incomplete infusion due to the COVID-19 pandemic; the
patient completed the rest of the treatment cycles and achieved a CR. Two other patients
were administered mosunetuzumab at a faster infusion rate than recommended per
protocol. Both patients achieved CR as their best overall response.

A total of 11 major protocol deviations due to the COVID-19 pandemic were reported in
10 patients in Group B overall. None of the 5 major protocol deviations due to COVID-19 in the
pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort were attributed to incomplete/missing/out-of-window tumor
assessments and therefore, response rates and the TTE analyses are not expected to be
impacted by the missing data due to the COVID-19 pandemic.
Overall, none of the major protocol deviations were considered to have impacted the
monitoring of patient safety, the study outcome, or the interpretation of the study results.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of protocol deviations.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of demographic characteristics in the FL cohort
of 90 patients. As noted in the Applicant’s position, the majority of patients were White (82%)
with limited representation from other races and ethnicities. In particular, there was an
underrepresentation of Black or African American patients and Hispanic or Latino patients, who
comprised only 4% and 8% of the primary FL population, respectively. In Section 2.1, the
Applicant stated that the incidence of FL in Black or African American patients was 4%. This
incidence was based on 70,882 patients with follicular lymphoma. Of these patients with
follicular lymphoma, an incidence of 10% in Hispanic or Latino patients was observed. The FL
cohort from study GO29781 has a sample size of 90 patients, therefore the similar incidence of
Black or African American and Hispanic or Latino patients is influenced by the small sample size
of the FL cohort.

Accountability and study treatment compliance, as required per protocol, were assessed by

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.

(b)

In the updated analysis based on a CCOD of 27 August 2021, the CR rate as assessed by IRF in
the B11 FL RP2D cohort was 60.0% (95% CI: 49.1, 70.2) (54/90 patients).
