Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
The Applicant’s Position:
Investigator-assessed CR rate
Investigator-assessed CR rate in the B11 FL RP2D cohort was 60.0% (95% CI: 49.1, 70.2) the
same as the IRF-assessed CR rate. Concordance between IRF and Investigator on whether a
patient achieved a CR was 93.3%.
Objective Response Rate
The objective response rate (ORR; PR or CR) by IRF assessment was 80.0% (95% CI: 73.0, 87.7)
and consistent with the Investigator-assessed ORR rate of 77.8% (95% CI: 67.8, 85.9).
Concordance between IRF and investigator on whether a patient achieved a CR or PR was
95.5%.
Duration of Complete Response
Of 54 patients in the pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort who achieved a CR as assessed by the IRF,
16 patients (29.6%) subsequently had disease progression by the time of the CCOD. The median
duration of complete response (DOCR) was not estimable. The K-M -estimated event-free rate
among complete responders at 12 and 18 months after the first CR was 71.4% and 63.7%,
respectively. Similar results were observed for DOCR by investigator assessment, with 80.4%
and 66.6% of complete responders being event-free at 12 and 18 months, respectively, after
the first CR (Table 21).
Duration of Response
The median duration of follow-up for DOR (by IRF) estimated using the reverse K-M method
was 14.9 months (95% CI: 13.4, 16.6). At the CCOD, of the responders, 87.5% had either
ongoing responses of at least 9 months or had progressed or died, 9.7% had DOR of <9 months
but no longer had tumor assessments, and the remaining 2.8% had ongoing responses which
continued to be assessed.
Of the 72 patients in the B11 FL RP2D cohort who achieved a response (CR or PR) as determined
by the IRF, 29 patients (40.3%) had subsequently progressed or died. The K-M estimated
median DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 9.7, NE), however this estimate was based on <10% of
105
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
responders remaining at risk and therefore may not be robust. Among responders, the eventfree rates at 12 and 18 months after the first response were 61.8% and 56.9%, respectively.
Similar results were observed for DOR by investigator assessment, with 64.8% and 62.5% of
responders being event-free at 12 and 18 months, respectively, after the first response (Table
21). Concordance between the IRF and the investigator in DOR was 74.2%.
In the subgroup of responders in the B11 FL RP2D cohort who achieved a CR by IRF assessment
(54 patients), an estimated 76.4% (95% CI: 64.6, 88.1) maintained their response at 12 months
after the first response. For DOR in patients who achieved a CR by investigator assessment,
84.3% were event-free at 12 months.
Progression-free Survival
At the time of the CCOD, 46.7% of patients in the B11 FL RP2D cohort had an IRF-assessed PFS
event. The K-M estimated median PFS was 17.9 months (95% CI: 10.1, NE). The 12-month PFS
event-free rate was 57.7% (95% CI: 46.9, 68.4). Overall, consistent results were observed with
INV-assessed PFS.
Overall Survival
Survival data was not mature at the CCOD, with 8 deaths reported (8.9%) in the B11 FL RP2D
cohort. The K-M estimated 12- and 18-month survival rates were 93.0% (95% CI: 87.6, 98.4) and
89.6% (95% CI: 82.5, 96.6), respectively.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. As noted in the Applicant’s position,
the median DOR for ORR was 22.8 months, however this estimate was based on <10% of
responders remaining at risk. This is coupled with the median follow-up for DOR of 14.9
months. Therefore, to provide additional context for DOR, the landmark assessment of
continued response at 12 months with 62% and at 18 months with 57% is supportive of
durability. Additionally, FDA considers the time to event endpoints, including progression-free
survival and overall survival, as not interpretable in single-arm trials. Therefore, the
corresponding analysis of progression-free survival and overall survival results presented in
Table 21 are considered exploratory analyses.
Dose/Dose Response
The Applicant’s Position:
The Exposure-Response (E-R) relationships for efficacy were characterized based on INVassessed clinical objective response data from 159 patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies who
were treated with single agent mosunetuzumab by IV administration in Study GO29781 (see
Exposure-Response Report No. 1112149). These patients received mosunetuzumab doses
106
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
ranging from 0.2 to 1/2/60/30 mg (7 patients from Group A q3w fixed-dose escalation cohorts
and 152 patients from Group B step-up dosing cohort), yielding a 600-fold range in
mosunetuzumab exposures (AUC0-42). The E-R analyses indicate that clinical responses (CR rate
and ORR) increase with increasing mosunetuzumab exposure (AUC0-42) and approach a plateau
estimated by the model as the maximal clinical responses of 68.6% and 81.4% for CR rate and
ORR, respectively. The model predictions for the proposed 1/2/60/30 mg regimen are at the
plateau of the logistic regression Emax E-R curves, while lower doses had reduced CR rate and
ORR predictions. The rationale for the intended registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg
based on integrated E-R analyses of efficacy and safety for Study GO29781 is summarized in
Section 6.2.2.1.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with Applicant’s position. Refer to Section 6.2, 6.3, and Section 20.4.5 for
additional information.
Durability of Response
The Applicant’s Position:
Objective responses occurring during mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy were durable, and
extended beyond the fixed treatment duration of 8 or 17 q3w cycles (see Duration of Response.
under Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints). With median follow-up for
DOR of 14.9 months, the 18-month event-free rate was 56.9% (95% CI: 44.1, 69.6) for all
responders, and 70.2% (95% CI: 56.7, 83.8) for patients who achieved a CR, indicating that
patients who achieve a CR may have a higher probability of remission lasting at least 1.5 years.
The durability of response to mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy is further supported by the
results from 46 patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies treated at the B7 interim dose level
(1.0/2.0/13.5 mg). The median follow-up for DOR in this lower dose cohort was 24.2 months
[95% CI: 21.4, 28.3]), and the 18-month DOR rate (54.0% [95% CI: 35.5, 72.4]) was similar to the
B11 FL RP2D cohort.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position and the results of duration of response and
duration of CR presented above. The median follow-up time for DOR was 14.9 months (95% CI:
13.4, 16.8) among 72 responders who achieved CR or PR. The median follow-up time for
duration of CR was 13.6 months (95% CI: 10.8, 16.3) among 54 responders who achieved CR.
Persistence of Effect
The Applicant’s Position:
107
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
Response to mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy occurs early in the majority of responders
(median time to response is 1.4 months for all responses and 3.0 months for CRs). The
durability of responses beyond the fixed treatment period of 8 cycles (5.5 months) for patients
with CR and 17 cycles (11.8 months) for patients with PR or SD provides evidence for the
persistence of mosunetuzumab efficacy over time.
The long-term persistency of the treatment effect of mosunetuzumab is not established,
however the time-to-event endpoint K-M estimates at the primary efficacy analysis can be
appreciated in the context of the disease setting and patient population under study. Although
direct comparison of longer term benefit of mosunetuzumab IV treatment with an appropriate
control is not possible in the confines of a single arm study and the lack of a comparator that
would be considered the standard of care, the estimated 61.8% of patients who continued to
benefit from remission after 12 months compares favorably with most approved therapies for
patients with R/R FL who have received with ≥2 prior therapies treated for whom median DOR
is approximately a year or less (see Table 22). Furthermore, the 12-month PFS event-free rate
of 57.7% (95% CI: 46.9, 68.4) compares favorably with published real-world estimates of
median PFS ranging from 1-1.1 year for third-line patients (represented at 37.8% in the B11 FL
RP2D cohort) decreasing to 0.5 years for sixth-line patients (Alperovich et al. 2016; Batlevi et al.
2020).
A lack of tolerance effects is indicated by patients who received retreatment with
mosunetuzumab. Of the 11 patients with R/R FL who received mosunetuzumab IV
monotherapy retreatment for relapsed disease after an initial response of CR, 7 patients had an
objective response to retreatment (5 CRs and 2 PRs) with duration of response to retreatment
ranging from 5.3-20.7 months (ongoing at CCOD).
As no ADAs were detected in any patient (see Section 8.2.9) there is no evidence to support the
potential impact of neutralizing antibodies as a theoretical mode of tolerance or resistance to
therapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. However, the comparison of time to
events endpoints (e.g., the percentages of patients who continued to be in remission at 12
months, and PFS at 12 months) between this single-arm trial and select published literature and
the conclusion that such a comparison is in favor of mosunetuzumab is not well established.
Generally, FDA does not consider any inferential procedures to evaluate time to event
endpoints in single-arm trials and considers these results to be descriptive and hypothesis
generating only.
Efficacy Results – Secondary or exploratory COA (PRO) endpoints
108
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
The Applicant’s Position:
PRO-based assessments of HRQoL in patients in the NHL expansion cohorts were introduced as
a secondary endpoint in protocol v6, prior to the first patient being enrolled in the expansion
cohorts.
At baseline, patients in the R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies expansion cohorts reported normal
physical functioning and slightly elevated baseline fatigue symptom scores of the EORTC
QLQ-C30 questionnaire, minimal to some burden of lymphoma-specific symptoms and concerns
as measured by the FACT-Lym subscale, and a low level of health status impairment at baseline
as measured by the EQ-5D-5L index utility scores and VAS. Changes from baseline scores at PRO
assessment visits during the first 8 cycles of initial treatment were highly variable between
patients, however mean changes from baseline by visit were not clinically meaningful indicating
that baseline HRQoL and health status across the cohort populations as a whole was
maintained during treatment with single-agent mosunetuzumab.
In the B11 FL RP2D cohort, clinically meaningful improvements compared to baseline
(≥10-point for EORT QLQ-C30 and ≥3-point for FACT-Lym) were observed for physical
functioning score in up to 15.8% of patients, fatigue score in up to 37.9% of patients, and for
lymphoma symptoms in up to 44.0% of patients at specific PRO assessment visits during the
8 cycles of initial treatment with mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
For B11 FL RP2D cohort, the compliance rates were >85% until Cycle 8 Day 1 for all three
questionnaires and remained ≥75% afterwards. The FDA acknowledges the descriptive
improvements for physical function, fatigue, and lymphoma symptoms. However, the FDA
disagrees with the statement that the changes were clinically meaningful. For patient-reported
disease improvement, a comparator arm along with a prespecified PRO endpoint and analysis
plan are necessary for adequate interpretation. Additionally, the single-arm, open-label trial
design limits the PRO data interpretation since a patients’ knowledge of the treatment regimen
may lead to systematic overestimation or underestimation of the treatment effect.
Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
Data:
CR rate and ORR by Subgroup
Subgroup analyses of the CR rate and ORR by IRF assessment demonstrated the consistency of
the treatment effect across relevant subpopulations defined by demographic (gender, age
range categories, race/ethnicity, ECOG PS), baseline disease (bulky disease), prior treatment
(number of prior systemic therapies, and refractory status to those prior treatments), and
prognostic factors (FLIPI risk factor). CR rate and ORR for all subgroups were consistent with the
response rates of the overall cohort. No major differences were observed among the
109
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
subgroups. Specifically, consistent CR rates were observed in high-risk patient populations,
including those with double refractory disease (50%), those who progressed within 24 months
of initial treatment (57%), and those with disease refractory to PI3K inhibitors (50%).
Time to Response
Among the 72 patients who achieved an objective response (CR or PR) by IRF in the
B11 FL RP2D cohort, the median time to first response was 1.4 months (range: 1.1−8.9 months),
while median time to first CR in the 54 patients who achieved a CR (by IRF) was 3.0 months
(range: 1.1−18.9 months).
Response Beyond 8 Cycles of Mosunetuzumab IV Monotherapy
A late initial response or deepening of a response was observed in some patients who had SD or
PR per Investigator assessment at the end of Cycle 8 and who received further mosunetuzumab
treatment beyond 8 cycles. Of the 16 patients who received >8 cycles of mosunetuzumab IV
monotherapy in the B11 FL RP2D cohort, 14 patients had tumor assessments beyond Cycle 8.
Of these 14 patients, 6 patients who had a best response of SD or PR by the end of Cycle 8 had
a late initial response or deepening of response (1 SD→PR, 5 PR→CR), 6 patients maintained SD
(3) or their PR (3), and 1 patient progressed (SD→PD)(One other patient received
mosunetuzumab doses beyond Cycle 8 despite having a CR at the end of Cycle 8 [captured as a
major protocol deviation]). These data indicate the benefit of continuing mosunetuzumab IV
monotherapy treatment beyond 8 cycles for patients who demonstrate disease control but
have not yet achieved a complete response at Cycle 8.
Time to Next Treatment
Time to next treatment (TTNT) was defined as the time from the end of study treatment to the
start of new anti-lymphoma treatment (NALT) or death. Thirty patients in the B11 FL RP2D
cohort started NALT and 3 additional patients had died prior to starting NALT (33 events,
36.7%). The K-M–estimated median TTNT in the B11 FL RP2D cohort was not estimable. The
12–month event-free rate was 68.1% (95% CI: 58.3, 77.9).
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position and the above results of subgroup
analyses, time to response, response beyond 8 cycles of treatment, and time to next treatment.
There were a limited number of patients (N = 16) that achieved a PR or SD with the initial 8
cycles of mosunetuzumab and received additional treatment (up to 17 cycles). As noted in the
Applicant’s position, of 14 patients with response assessments beyond Cycle 8, 6 patients had a
deepening of response and 6 patients maintained their initial responses. In the context of
limited patient numbers, this data provides some preliminary supportive evidence for
continuation of treatment with mosunetuzumab beyond 8 cycles for patients that achieve a PR
or SD with initial therapy.

Integrated Review of Effectiveness
The FDA’s Assessment:
Not applicable. See Sections below.
Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
Not applicable as efficacy to support the application is based on the single pivotal study
GO29781.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The efficacy analysis is solely based on Study
GO29781 and an integrated summary of efficacy was not included in the application. FDA notes
that Study GO29781 was adequate to support the efficacy of the mosunetuzumab IV
monotherapy in the indicated population.
Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment with mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy induced durable complete remission in the
majority of patients with R/R FL who had received at least two prior anti-lymphoma therapies,
including patients who were refractory to multiple classes of prior treatment and with poor
prognostic factors.
In the primary efficacy analysis based on a CCOD of 15 March 2021, the IRF-assessed CR rate of
57.8% (95% CI: 46.9, 68.1) with mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy at the intended registration
(b)
dose at that time was statistically significantly greater than a historical control CR rate of (4) %
for patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies (exact binomial test, two-sided p value <0.0001)
based on CR rates reported for copanlisib in the CHRONOS-1 study (Dreyling et al. 2017), and
compares favorably with other approved therapies (Table 22).
Compared with therapies specifically approved to treat patients with R/R FL after 2 or 3 prior
systemic therapies, the updated IRF-assessed CR rate based on a CCOD of 27 August 2021
(60.0% [95% CI: 49.1, 70.2]) and ORR 80.0% (95% CI: 70.3, 87.7) achieved by mosunetuzumab is
greater than the respective response rates for the PI3K inhibitors idelalisib, copanlisib,
duvelisib, and umbralisib, and the EZH2 inhibitor tazemetostat (CR range: 1−14%; ORR range:
34−69%; Table 22). The difference in response rate with mosunetuzumab appeared clinically
meaningful as the lower bound 95% CI for the IRF-assessed CR rate (49.1%) and ORR (70.3%)
exclude the reported point estimates for the respective response rates for the PI3K inhibitors.
In addition, the K-M estimated median DOR in the pivotal B11 FL RP2D cohort of GO29781 was
111
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
22.8 months (95% CI: 9.7, NE), however this estimate was based on <10% of responders
remaining at risk and therefore may not be robust. Among responders, an estimated 56.9%
remained in remission at 18 months after the first response, which compares favorably with
median DORs ranging from 10 to 13 months for these approved therapies (Table 22). The
proportion of patients who achieved CR and the durability of responses for patients with R/R FL
≥2 prior therapies treated with mosunetuzumab approach the results of axicabtagene
ciloleucel, a CAR-T therapy (60% CR, 91% ORR; Table 22). While CAR-T therapies have shown
efficacy, their use may be limited by treatment delay associated with the ex-vivo T-cell
manipulation (median time from leukapheresis to product infusion 27 days [range 19 to 250
days]) and complex CAR-T manufacturing, which may be prohibitive in patients with rapidly
progressing disease, as well as the high incidence of CRS and neurological adverse events (see
Section 8.2.11). Of available therapies approved for first- and/or second-line FL which can be
used as third-line and subsequent therapy, the CR rates for the combinations of rituximab plus
lenalidomide (R-Len) and obinutuzumab plus bendamustine (G-B) in the third-line R/R FL setting
(13-34% and 20%, respectively) are lower than for mosunetuzumab, while objective responses
(57%-81% and 75%, respectively) are similar (Table 22; FDA Multidisciplinary Review and
Evaluation of UKONIQ® and TAZVERIK®). However, the studies which supported the regular
approval of R-Len (MAGNIFY and AUGMENT) and G-B (GADOLIN) enrolled earlier-line, less
refractory, and less heavily pretreated patients with R/R FL than the GO29781 study. The
highest response rates with R-Len were observed in AUGMENT, which enrolled patients
without rituximab-refractory disease, compared to MAGNIFY (47% rituximab-refractory for
patients with FL ≥2 prior therapies) whereas 79% of patients in GO29781 were refractory to a
prior anti-CD20 therapy. Compared to R-Len in patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies in
MAGNIFY, the DOR for mosunetuzumab appears longer. The response rates for G-B in GADOLIN
in the third-line setting were based on patients with rituximab-refractory FL who had received a
median of 2 (range 2-10) prior therapies whereas in GO29781, patients had received a median
of 3 (2-10) prior therapies.
The deep and durable responses to single agent mosunetuzumab are clinically meaningful and
may demonstrate advantages over approved therapies for the treatment of patients with
R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies, a patient population with a high unmet medical need, who have
rapidly progressing disease and need urgent disease control. To confirm the clinical benefit, the
Applicant is conducting a randomized Phase III Study GO42909 to evaluate mosunetuzumab in
combination with lenalidomide vs. rituximab in combination with lenalidomide in patients with
FL after at least one line of systemic therapy with the primary efficacy endpoint of PFS.
Study GO42909 is ongoing and enrolled the first patient on 27 October 2021.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
The FDA’s Assessment:
The efficacy of mosunetuzumab IV was based on GO29781, an open-label, multi-cohort, singlearm trial that included a cohort of 90 adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular
lymphoma who had received at least two prior therapies, including an anti-CD20 monoclonal
antibody and an alkylating agent. The study excluded patients with active infections, history of
autoimmune disease, prior allogeneic transplant, or any history of CNS lymphoma or CNS
disorders.
Patients received mosunetuzumab at step-up doses of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 2 mg on Cycle
1 Day 8, followed by 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 60 mg on Cycle 2 Day 1, then 30 mg every 3
weeks in subsequent cycles. A treatment cycle was 21 days. Mosunetuzumab was administered
for 8 cycles unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. After 8
cycles, patients with a complete response discontinued therapy; patients with a partial
response or stable disease continued treatment up to 17 cycles, unless patients experienced
progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity.
The prespecified primary endpoint was complete response rate per independent review facility
per the 2007 international working group criteria (Cheson 2007).
Among the 90 patients with relapsed or refractory FL, the median age was 60 years (range:
29 to 90 years), 33% were 65 years of age or older, 61% were male, 82% were White, 9% were
Asian, 4% were Black or African American, and 8% were Hispanic or Latino. A total of 77% of
patients had Stage III-IV disease, 34% had bulky disease, and all patients had an ECOG
performance status of 0 or 1. The median number of prior therapies was 3 (range: 2 to 10), with
38% receiving 2 prior therapies, 31% receiving 3 prior therapies, and 31% receiving more than 3
prior therapies.
Seventy-nine percent of patients were refractory to prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody
therapy, 53% were refractory to both anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and alkylator therapy, 9%
received prior rituximab plus lenalidomide therapy, 21% received prior autologous stem cell
transplant, and 3% received prior CAR T therapy. Fifty-two percent of patients had progression
of disease within 24 months of first systemic therapy.
Although CR rate is pre-specified as the primary endpoint in the protocol and statistical analysis
plan, FDA considers the intermediate endpoint of ORR and duration of response (DOR) as
assessed by an independent review facility according to standard criteria for NHL (Cheson 2007)
as the basis for efficacy. For the B11 FL RP2D expansion cohort of 90 patients with FL, the ORR
rate was 80% (95% CI: 70.3%, 87.7%), with 60% achieving a complete response. The median
DOR per Kaplan-Meier method was 22.8 months (95% CI: 9.7, not estimable) with a median
DOR follow-up of 14.9 months. Given the median DOR follow-up and that the KM estimate was
based on <10% of responders remaining at risk, further support for DOR was assessed using the
115
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
landmark assessments of continued response at 12 months with 62% and at 18 months with
57%.
Based on the characteristics of the FL patient population enrolled in the expansion cohort of
GO29781, the data support the determination that mosunetuzumab has clinically meaningful
activity in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of
systemic therapy. Some limitations of the efficacy data include the limited racial and ethnic
representation with 82% White, 9% Asian 4% Black or African American, and 8% Hispanic or
Latino. Further characterization of mosunetuzumab in a broader ethnic and racial population is
warranted and informed a postmarketing requirement. In addition, the proposed treatment
regimen is to continue treatment in patients that achieve a PR or SD within the initial 8 cycles of
therapy. The data for treatment beyond 8 cycles is limited with only 16 patients. Although, 6
patients had a deepening of response and 6 patients had a maintenance of response with
continued treatment. The proposed treatment regimen is further supported by the data with
duration of response. Nevertheless, continued evaluation of the long-term efficacy outcomes
remains warranted.
For information on the efficacy data in the context of available therapy for patients with
relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after at least 2 prior therapies, see Section 8.4.

Review of Safety
Data:
The safety database included 447 patients in the safety-evaluable population of Study GO29781
defined as all patients in Group A and Group B who received at least one dose of
mosunetuzumab (as a single agent by IV administration). The primary safety analysis set
comprised of 218 patients in the Group B expansion cohorts treated at the RP2D/intended
registration dose and schedule of 1/2/60/30 mg (referred to as B11 RP2D), and includes
90 patients with R/R FL ≥2 prior therapies (referred to as B11 FL RP2D) and other histologyspecific cohorts (88 DLBCL/trFL, 25 MCL, and 14 Richter’s transformation; one patient with
melanoma who was enrolled in error in the R/R DLBCL/trFL RP2D expansion cohort and
included in safety analyses because they received one dose of mosunetuzumab treatment). In
addition to the 218 patients treated with the registration dosing regimen, an additional
196 patients treated at other doses in Group B (Cycle 1 step-up) dose escalation and interim
expansion cohorts (414 patients in total of mixed NHL histologies; referred as Group B) were
included in the safety database to provide a comprehensive overview of the safety profile of
single agent mosunetuzumab IV using the Cycle 1 step-up dosing schedule. Safety data from
33 patients with R/R NHL initially treated with escalating doses of mosunetuzumab IV
monotherapy administered on a q3w fixed dosing schedule (referred as Group A) were also
analyzed to complete the safety evaluation of mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy and to support
exposure-response (E-R) modeling analyses and justification of RP2D determination. As the

dosing schedule was different, and patients in Group A received doses of mosunetuzumab per

cohort), Group A was analyzed separately from Group B (see Table 23).
The safety review is of data from Study GO29781 collected up to a clinical cutoff of
27 August 2021, and is an update to the primary analysis based on an earlier CCOD of
15 March 2021.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA’s safety review was primarily focused on the 218 patients treated at the RP2D
(1/2/60/30 mg) in Study GO29781, which includes the primary follicular lymphoma population
of 90 patients, with a data cutoff of August 27, 2021. The safety data from the additional 196
patients with NHL (Group B) treated at different doses or schedules from the RP2D were
considered supportive.
Safety Review Approach
The Applicant’s Position:
The focus of the safety review in Study GO29781 was to establish the safety profile of
mosunetuzumab in R/R NHL patients, including R/R FL patients who received ≥2 prior therapies.
The safety profile of single agent mosunetuzumab was assessed by analyzing the incidence and
severity of all treatment-emergent AEs, serious AEs (including Grade 5 AEs), adverse events of
special interest (AESIs), AEs leading to discontinuation, AEs leading to dose modification (dose
reduction or interruption), clinical laboratory test abnormalities (hematology and chemistry),
electrocardiograms (ECGs), vital signs, and anti-drug antibodies (ADAs).
Treatment-emergent AEs were defined as AEs with onset on the day of or after first
administration of study treatment. After the initiation of the study drug, all AEs with an onset
date between the first dose of study drug and 90 days after the last administration of study
drug or initiation of another anti-cancer treatment, whichever occurred first, were reported.
After this AE reporting period, only SAEs assessed to be related to prior study treatment were
reported. Per protocol, all deaths attributed to progression of disease were to be reported as
an AE (preferred term malignant neoplasm progression) if the event occurred during the
protocol-specified reporting period.

Information on categorization of AEs is provided in Section 8.2.3.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Safety analyses were conducted on the complete datasets provided by the Applicant with an
August 27, 2021 data cutoff. The primary safety population was comprised of the 218 patients

with NHL in the group B dose expansion cohort of study GO29781 who received step-up dosing
during cycle 1 of mosunetuzumab monotherapy at the RP2D of 1/2/60/30 mg. This data pool
provided an adequate number of patients for a review of safety.
Ninety patients with follicular lymphoma in the B11 expansion cohort were analyzed separately
to inform safety in the proposed indication in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular
lymphoma.
The clinical review of safety for this BLA is based on the following:
- Clinical study report for study GO29781
- Protocol and statistical analysis plan for study GO29781
- Case report forms and safety narratives
- Summary of clinical safety
- Proposed labeling for mosunetuzumab
The Applicant reported adverse events using single MedDRA preferred terms (PTs). For
increased sensitivity, FDA used a combination of grouped PTs, as defined in Appendix 19.5.
Adverse events that involve more than one body system were consolidated and reported under
the most commonly involved or most appropriate body system. Unless noted, all presented
analyses use the FDA grouped PTs.
The Applicant reported treatment emergent deaths due to progressive disease as an adverse
event per protocol. For FDA analyses, treatment emergent deaths due to progressive disease
are categorized as death due to progressive disease and are not considered an adverse event.

The Applicant’s Position:
Single agent mosunetuzumab administered IV by Cycle 1 step-up dosing was evaluated in
196 patients with R/R NHL the Group B dose escalation at doses ranging from 0.4/1/2.8 mg to
1/2/60 mg (dose administered on C1D1/C1D8/C1D15, C2D1 and subsequent q3w cycles) and
218 patients at the proposed registration dose identified as the RP2D and schedule (1 mg on
C1D1, 2 mg on C1D8, 60 mg on C1D15 and C2D1, and 30 mg for C3D1 and subsequent q3w
cycles; see Section 6.2.2.1). Mosunetuzumab treatment was administered for a fixed duration,
up to a maximum of 8 cycles for patients who achieved a CR after 8 cycles (approximately
5.5 months), or extended to 17 cycles for patients who achieved a PR or maintained stable
disease after 8 cycles (approximately one year).
Patients in the B11 RP2D cohort received a median of 8 cycles (range: 1−17) of mosunetuzumab
IV monotherapy treatment. Most patients received their planned doses of mosunetuzumab;
the median dose intensity was 99.4% and 81.7% of patients achieved a dose intensity of >90%.
The median duration of treatment of mosunetuzumab was 4.9 months (range 0.03−13.8),
which corresponds approximately to the median of 8 x q3w cycles received. At the time of the
CCOD, the median observation time in the B11 RP2D cohort was 14.3 months (range:
0.1−27.9 months).
Cumulative exposure to mosunetuzumab in Group B was less than in the B11 R2PD cohort, due
to fewer median number of cycles received and the lower doses used in the dose escalation
portion of the study to establish the RP2D. Generally, the extent of exposure to
mosunetuzumab in the B11 FL RP2D cohort was similar to the B11 RP2D cohort, with the
exception of a higher proportion of patients in the B11 FL RP2D cohort that received the full
119
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
8 cycles (58.9%) of treatment compared to the B11 RP2D cohort (36.7%), due to lower rates of
early disease progression in the patients with R/R FL.
Overall, the extent of exposure to mosunetuzumab in Study GO29781 is consistent with the
expected exposure and treatment duration for the indicated patient population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA’s safety review was focused on the 218 patients treated at the RP2D in Study G029781
(referred to as the primary safety population). The proposed dosing regimen was
mosunetuzumab administered at 1.0 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 (C1D1), 2.0 mg on C1D8, 60 mg on
C1D15 and C2D1, and 30 mg for subsequent doses of a 21-day cycle. The median exposure
duration for patients in the B11 RP2D cohort and FL cohort was similar, 4.9 months each.
Eighty-one to eighty-two percent of patients achieved a relative dose intensity of >90% in the FL
cohort and the B11 RP2D cohort. A larger percentage of patients received 8 cycles of therapy in
the B11 FL cohort as compared to the B11 RP2D cohort which likely reflects the heterogeneity
of the histologies included in the RP2D cohort, including aggressive NHL histologies. The safety
data provided for patients treated beyond cycles 8 is limited for both cohorts.

Adequacy of the safety database:
The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant believes that the safety analysis set of 414 patients with R/R NHL including
218 patients treated with the registration dosing regimen (of which 90 patients with R/R FL ≥2
Prior Therapies represented the patient population for the proposed indication) is sufficient to
adequately evaluate the incidence of toxicities and to support registration of mosunetuzumab
for the proposed indication.
The adequacy of the total patient exposure of mosunetuzumab to support registration in the
target population was discussed at a Type B End-of-Phase I meeting (17 March 2020. Ref ID:
4577186). The FDA considered the planned safety database at that time to be reasonable.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The size of the extended safety database of 414 patients with NHL who received
mosunetuzumab monotherapy, the size of the primary safety database of 218 patients with
NHL who received mosunetuzumab at the RP2D, and the size of the primary efficacy population
of 90 patients with FL are considered adequate for review.

The Applicant’s Position:
No issues relating to safety data integrity or quality were identified for Study GO29781.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The quality of the safety data submitted was

Categorization of Adverse Event
The Applicant’s Position:
Verbatim descriptions for AEs entered by the investigators were assigned to preferred terms
(PTs) by using MedDRA Version 24.0. AE severity (with the exception of CRS events) and routine
laboratory parameters were graded using the NCI CTCAE v4.0. AEs were then presented by PT
and system organ class (SOC).
Dedicated analyses were conducted for adverse events of special interest (AESI) specific for
mosunetuzumab based on evolving clinical experience in clinical studies. The AESI categories
were cytokine release syndrome (CRS), neurologic AEs and a subset of driving-impacting
cognition or consciousness neurologic adverse event (DI-CCNAEs), hematologic AEs,
hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis (HLH), tumor lysis syndrome, tumor flare, hepatic AEs,
infections, and pneumonitis or interstitial lung disease (as requested by the FDA at the Type B
Pre-BLA meeting held on 21 January 2021, to discuss content and format. Ref ID: 4749002).
Search definitions used were based on either singular MedDRA Preferred Term [PT] or multiple
PTs under defined SMQs, SOCs or specific adverse grouped terms as detailed in Table 3 of the
GO29781 CSRs.
Per protocol, investigators graded CRS events according to the Lee 2014 grading criteria (Lee et
al. 2014). CRS events based on the ASTCT 2019 grading criteria were derived programmatically
from the reported data, based on the presence of fever and the presence and management of
hypotension or hypoxia as reported in the CRS signs/symptoms eCRF (Lee et al. 2019). CRS
events by both Lee 2014 and ASTCT 2019 grading are reported in the clinical study reports for
the GO29781 study supporting this application. The summary of safety results presented in
Section 8.2.4 below describe CRS events according to ASTCT 2019 grading criteria.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. All adverse events and serious adverse events
were captured during the trial and for up to 90 days after the last dose of study treatment or
until the initiation of another systemic anti-cancer therapy, whichever occurred first. The FDA’s
125
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
review of safety is focused on treatment emergent adverse events (TEAE) that occurred within
30 days of the last dose of study treatment. Given the single-arm trial design and the absence
of a comparator arm, FDA considers all TEAEs regardless of investigator attribution.
Routine Clinical Tests
The Applicant’s Position:
Routine clinical tests included physical examinations, laboratory tests, ECGs and vital signs.
A complete physical exam was scheduled at screening with targeted symptom-directed physical
exams schedule at subsequent visits. As part of the clinical examination, patients were routinely
assessed for any signs or symptoms of neurologic toxicity.
Laboratory tests for hematology, serum chemistry and creatinine, coagulation parameters, LDH,
C-reactive protein and serum ferritin were conducted at local laboratories at the study site.
Viral serology and detection was conducted at screening.
Triplicate 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) recordings were scheduled at screening (baseline),
before and at the end of infusion on Days 1 and 15 of Cycle 1 and before the infusion on
Cycle 4. ECGs were also performed when clinically indicated.
Vital signs (systolic, and diastolic BP, respiratory rate, pulse oximetry, pulse rate and body
temperature) were assessed before, during, at the end, and after infusions. In the event of a
suspected anaphylactic reaction during mosunetuzumab administration patients were
monitored with continuous electrocardiogram and pulse oximetry.
Specific tests for PK, ADAs, serum cytokines and other biomarkers in blood samples or other
biological samples were conducted at a central lab/specialty bioanalytics lab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The frequency of safety monitoring was
considered adequate in the context of the study.

The Applicant’s Position:
A total of 82 of 218 patients (37.6%) in the B11 RP2D cohort had died. Table 26 summarizes
deaths by cause. Progressive disease accounted for the majority of deaths (68/218 patients
[31.2%]). A higher proportion of PD-related deaths occurred in patients with DLBCL, MCL, and
Richter’s transformation than in patients with FL. For this reason, the lowest proportion of
deaths occurred in the B11 FL RP2D cohort (8/90 patients [8.9%]), than in the B11 RP2D cohort
(82/218 [37.6%] or Group B overall (174/414 [42.0%]).
Of the remaining 14 deaths in the B11 RP2D cohort, 10 deaths were in patients who had
discontinued mosunetuzumab treatment and occurred outside of protocol-specified AE
reporting period with the primary cause of death as “Other” entered on the Study
Completion/Early Discontinuation eCRF page. Specific details provided by the investigator are
described in the footnote (f) to Table 26. The other 4 deaths in the B11 RP2D cohort were due
to non-PD fatal AEs (i.e., other than malignant neoplasm progression, see below).
Grade 5 AEs were reported during the initial treatment with mosunetuzumab in 32/218 (14.7%)
patients in the B11 RP2D cohort. The majority (28/32) of Grade 5 AEs were disease progression
(PT of malignant neoplasm progression) occurring during the protocol-specified AE reporting
128
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
period. The 4 non-PD Grade 5 AEs were pneumonia, cholangitis, death ‘not otherwise specified’
(reported as PT of death; patient in B11 FL RP2D cohort), and sepsis (see Table 27).
Two additional non-PD Grade 5 AE of pneumonia and sepsis occurred in Group B at lower
mosunetuzumab doses. One other patient (in the Group A fixed-dose cohort) died due to a fatal
event of hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis (see Table 27).
The FDA’s Assessment:
Treatment emergent deaths were assessed up to 90 days post treatment discontinuation. For
safety, the FDA considers all-cause treatment emergent adverse events in the evaluation versus
those indicated as treatment-related by the Applicant. The FDA concurs that as of the August
27, 2021 data cutoff, a total of 82 deaths occurred within the B11 RP2D safety population, with
4 deaths occurring with mosunetuzumab due to an adverse event. The 4 deaths included
events of pneumonia, sepsis, cholangitis, and death unknown.
• For the event of death unknown, this represents a 66 year old African American male
with follicular lymphoma who experienced ASTCT grade 3 CRS, confusional state
(ICANS), and grade 4 acute kidney injury requiring continuous renal replacement
therapy by Day 27. The CRS, acute kidney injury, and confusional state all resolved by
Day 29. The patient was discharged from the hospital on Day 48, with a follow-up visit
on Day 56 demonstrating a normal exam and labs. On Day 60, the patient was reported
to have died due to unknown causes.
As noted in the Applicant’s position, 28 patients in the B11 RP2D cohort were reported to have
died due to malignant progression of disease while on treatment or within 90 days of last dose
(Table 27 above). However, one patient, a 74 year old White male with DLBCL developed Grade
1 confusional state on Day 7 and Grade 4 hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis (HLH) on Day 8.
Day 10 soluble IL2 receptor serum level was 20,000 pg/mL. By Day 13, the patient was reported
to show “clinical disease progression, worsening of encephalopathy, and multiorgan
dysfunction,” which appeared consistent with a clinical presentation of HLH. Mosunetuzumab
treatment was discontinued on Day 17 due to disease progression; however, the relative
contribution of mosunetuzumab to the development of HLH versus the contribution of
progressive disease to the development of HLH remains unclear. For additional information on
the evaluation of HLH with mosunetuzumab, see Section 8.2.4 below.
The 90 Day Safety Update provided safety data collected through January 3, 2022. No new fatal
AEs were reported in the B11 RP2D cohort in the safety update.

The Applicant’s Position:
Serious adverse events (SAE) including the Grade 5 AEs, were reported in 114/218 (52.3%) of
patients in the B11 RP2D cohort (Table 29). The most frequent SAEs (≥2% of patients) were CRS
(20.6% by ASTCT 2019 grade), malignant neoplasm progression (12.8% [all reported as Grade 5
AEs), pyrexia (4.6%), and pneumonia (3.2%). SAEs in the SOC of Infections and Infestations were
reported in 17.0% of patients.
The majority of patients with serious CRS events experienced Grade 1−2 events at the highest
grade. Serious pyrexia events were also predominantly Grade 1−2 and did not occur

pools. In the B11 RP2D cohort, 52% (114/218) experienced an SAE, most often due to CRS
(21%), pneumonia (5%), pyrexia (5%), sepsis (3%), and a 2% incidence each of tumor flare,
urinary tract infection, pleural effusion, and renal insufficiency.

The Applicant’s Position:
Adverse events led to the discontinuation of mosunetuzumab treatment in 9/218 patients
(4.1%) in the B11 RP2D cohort (Table 32). In 7 patients (3.2%) the AEs were serious and were
fatal events of sepsis and cholangitis (see Table 27), Grade 4 Epstein-Barr viremia, Grade 3 CRS
(ASTCT; Grade 4 by Lee 2014), subdural hematoma and myocardial infarction, and Grade 2 CRS.
At the RP2D, the two serious CRS events, were the only AEs that led to mosunetuzumab
discontinuation in more than one patient (both in the B11 FL RP2D cohort) and both events
resolved. The CRS, sepsis, and myocardial infarction events which led to treatment
discontinuation were considered to be related to mosunetuzumab by the investigator.
A similar proportion of patients in the B11 FL RP2D cohort (4.4%) and Group B (4.6%)
discontinued mosunetuzumab due to AEs compared to the B11 RP2D cohort.

The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of all-cause adverse events leading to
treatment discontinuation. For safety, the FDA considers all-cause treatment emergent adverse
events in the evaluation versus those indicated as treatment-related by the Applicant. The FDA
analysis of TEAEs in the B11 RP2D leading to treatment discontinuation is consistent with the
data presented in Table 32 above. Overall, treatment discontinuation due to a TEAE occurred in
4% (9/218). The most common adverse event leading to discontinuation was CRS.
Dose Interruption/Reduction Due to Adverse Effects
The Applicant’s Position:
Adverse events led to the mosunetuzumab dose being interrupted (delayed) or modified
(reduced) in 73/218 of patients (33.5%) in the B11 RP2D cohort. The most frequently reported
events (≥2% of patients) were neutropenia/neutrophil count decreased (25 patients [11.5%];
predominantly Grade 3 or 4), and CRS (17 patients [7.8%] by ASTCT 2019 grade; predominantly
Grade 1 or 2). Dose interruptions due to AEs were the most frequent action taken with
mosunetuzumab and in most cases the event resolved and patients were able to continue
mosunetuzumab treatment.
Per protocol, dose reductions in mosunetuzumab were permitted if it was determined by the
investigator that the clinical benefit may be maintained. A reduced dose was to be considered
for the next dose when resuming dosing following the occurrence and resolution of Grade 3
CRS, other study-treatment–related Grade 3 toxicity, serious adverse event, or a DLT.
Reductions in mosunetuzumab dose were infrequent, occurring in 7 patients (3.2%) in the B11
RP2D cohort and most frequently resulted from AEs with onset in Cycle 1. The AEs that led to
dose reductions in more than one patient were erythematous rash/rash and Grade ≥3 CRS.
The overall frequency and the most commonly reported AEs leading to dose interruption or
modification of mosunetuzumab were similar in the B11 RP2D cohort, B11 FL RP2D cohort, and
overall Group B.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The table below provides a summary
of the FDA analysis of adverse events leading to a dose interruption. In the B11 RP2D cohort of
218 patients, 33% experienced adverse events leading to dose interruption, most often due to
neutropenia, infection, and CRS.
For dose reductions, as noted by the Applicant, 3% (7/218) of patients in the B11 RP2D cohort
experienced an adverse event leading to dose reduction, most often due to CRS and rash.

The Applicant’s Position:
The majority of patients treated with mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy by Cycle 1 step-up
dosing at the intended registration dose and schedule experienced at least one AE of any grade
(see Table 34). Most AEs were Grade 1−2 in severity. The most common AE reported was CRS
(39.4% by ASTCT 2019 grade), which were predominantly Grade 1−2, limited primarily to
Cycle 1, and were manageable and reversible (all CRS events resolved). Other common AEs
(≥ 20% incidence) in the B11 RP2D cohort were fatigue, neutropenia/neutrophil count
decreased, pyrexia, hypophosphatemia, and headache.
By SOC, AEs were most frequently reported (≥50% patients) under General Disorders and
Administration Site Conditions (61.5%) driven mainly by fatigue and pyrexia, Skin and
Subcutaneous Tissue Disorders (56.4%) driven mainly by rash and pruritus, Gastrointestinal
Disorders (55.0%) driven mainly by diarrhea, constipation, and nausea, and Metabolism and
Nutrition Disorders (52.3%) driven mainly by hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia and
hypomagnesemia.
Overall, the nature, severity, and frequency of the most common AEs in the B11 RP2D cohort
were similar to those in B11 FL RP2D cohort, and overall Group B.
Grade 3-4 AEs were reported in 66.5% of patients in the Group B11 RP2D cohort (Table 34). The
most frequently reported Grade 3−4 AEs (≥5% in the B11 RP2D cohort) were
neutropenia/neutrophil count decreased (24.3%), hypophosphatemia (14.7%),
anemia/hemoglobin decreased (8.3%), thrombocytopenia/platelet count decreased (6.9%), and
hyperglycemia (5.5%). The majority of Grade 3-4 AEs were manageable with dose modification
or interruption of mosunetuzumab.
Based on the single-arm design of the GO29781 study, for the proposed label, ADRs from
mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy treatment were identified based on review of AEs (PTs or
grouped PTs) with incidence ≥10% (all grades) or ≥5% for Grade 3-4 based on the B11 RP2D
population (N=218). Events with an incidence of <10% (all grades) or <5% (Grade 3-4) could be
included if there was a medical rationale supporting the assessment (such as a known class
effect/known effect from mechanism of action), and at least one of the observed reports was

The Applicant’s Position:
Among the patients in the B11 RP2D cohort, the most frequent treatment-emergent Grade ≥3
worsening laboratory parameter shifts (≥10% of patients) were decreases in lymphocytes (the
pharmacodynamic action of mosunetuzumab to deplete B-cells), neutrophils, leukocytes,
hemoglobin, and platelets for hematology laboratory parameters, and decreases in phosphate,
increases in glucose and urate for chemistry laboratory parameters. These laboratory
abnormalities are consistent with neutropenia/neutrophil count decreased, anemia,
thrombocytopenia/platelet count decreased, hypophosphatemia, and hyperglycemia being the
most frequently reported Grade 3-4 AEs in the B11 RP2D cohort (see Section 6.14.1.2.2 and
6.14.2.2.2 of CSR).
One patient in B11 RP2D, and 10 patients in Group B overall, were identified as potential Hy’s
law cases due to laboratory results of >3xULN for AST or ALT and/or >2xULN for total bilirubin.
These liver enzyme abnormalities occurred in patients who later experienced disease
progression or occurred concurrently with CRS or as symptoms of CRS (see Section 6.14.2.2.1 of
CSR).

Data:
Across the safety population as a whole, there were no clinically relevant effects of
mosunetuzumab on heart rate, PR duration, QRS duration, QT duration, QTcB, QTcF, and RR
duration, as assessed by absolute or change from baseline values at scheduled ECG
assessments during the study.
The majority of ECG abnormalities were considered to be not clinically significant by the
investigator. Of 11 patients in Groups A and B who had clinically significant post-baseline ECG
abnormalities, 4 patients had ECG abnormalities at baseline.
Adverse events in the SOC of Cardiac Disorders were reported in 10.3% of patients (Group A
and B); most were Grade 1 or 2. Grade 3 events were reported in 4 patients, all in Group B
(atrial flutter, atrioventricular block second degree, and myocardial infarction (all serious), and
atrial fibrillation). In the 11 patients who had clinically significant post-baseline ECG
abnormalities, cardiac-related AEs were reported in 5 patients, and all were Grade 1 or 2.
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on the available data, there were no clinically relevant effects of mosunetuzumab on
cardiac function and cardiac disorders are not considered a risk for mosunetuzumab.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position and has provided additional information.
Triplicate ECG recordings were required at baseline, pre and post infusion on C1D1 and C1D15
and preinfusion on C4D1. Based on FDA grouping of related preferred terms in the B11 RP2D
cohort, ECG-related TEAEs in the SOC cardiac disorders included sinus tachycardia (4%),
tachycardia (2%), atrial fibrillation (2%), arrhythmia (<1%), bradycardia (<1%), and sinus
bradycardia (<1%). In the FL cohort, ECG-related TEAEs in the SOC cardiac disorders included
sinus tachycardia (3%), tachycardia (2%), and bradycardia (1%).
QT Assessment
The Applicant’s Position:
As a monoclonal antibody, mosunetuzumab is not expected to interact directly with the human
ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) channel and the risk for QT prolongation following
mosunetuzumab treatment is considered to be low. Clinical data indicate that mosunetuzumab
does not have a clinically relevant effect on QTcF interval across a wide dose range (0.05 to
60 mg) (see Electrocardiograms (ECGs) above). No dedicated QT study was conducted. The QT
assessment plan was discussed at the Type B Pre-BLA content and format meeting (21 January
2021. Ref ID: 4749002) where the Agency agreed with the Applicant’s proposal to not conduct a
dedicated QT study and that a separate QT evaluation report was not necessary.
The FDA’s Assessment:
147
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. A dedicated QT assessment was not required and
was not conducted as per ICH E14 Q&A (R3). However, 2 patients (<1%, Grade 1 and 2, one
patient each) developed “electrocardiogram QT prolongation” in the B11 RP2D cohort, both
less than 500 ms.
Immunogenicity
Data:
As of the CCOD used for the ADA analyses (4 December 2020), no anti-drug antibodies (ADAs)
were detected in any patient at any assessment in the 418 ADA-evaluable patients who
received mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy in Study GO29781.
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on the available data, the impact of immunogenicity on safety is low.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.
Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
8.2.4.1 CRS
Data:
In the B11 RP2D cohort, CRS events by ASTCT 2019 grade were reported in 39.4% of patients.
The majority were Grade 1−2 severity (95.1% [117/123 CRS events]). Grade 3 CRS events were
reported in 5 patients (2.3%) and Grade 4 CRS event was reported in a single patient (0.5%)
diagnosed with FL in leukemic phase. Serious CRS events were reported in 20.6% patients in the
B11 RP2D cohort (see Table 29); the majority were Grade 1−2 at maximum severity and
considered serious predominantly due to the requirement for hospitalization or prolonged
hospitalization.
CRS events occurred predominantly in Cycle 1 and were mainly associated with Day 1 and
Day 15 dose administrations, with the highest frequency of CRS events driven by Grade 1 and 2
events following the C1D15 dose in B11 RP2D. The frequency of CRS events decreased in the
subsequent treatment cycles. The 6 Grade 3-4 CRS events, all of which were serious, followed
C1D15 and C2D1 infusions. The median duration of CRS events was 3 days (range: 1-29 days)
Most CRS events were managed with supportive care (i.e., symptomatic management of
constitutional symptoms), with or without anti-IL-6/corticosteroid therapy. CRS events
requiring dose interruption/modification were reported in 17 patients (7.8%) in the B11 RP2D
cohort while CRS events that led to mosunetuzumab treatment discontinuation were reported
in 2 patients (0.9%; see Table 32). All CRS events resolved.
148
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
The most common treatments administered for any grade CRS were fluids (for hypotension),
steroids, tocilizumab, and low flow oxygen (for hypoxia). By ASTST grade:
•

Of 80 Grade 1 ASTCT CRS events reported in 61 patients in the B11 RP2D cohort, most
events (66/80 [82.5%]) were not treated with tocilizumab, corticosteroids, fluids,
vasopressors, or oxygen. Tocilizumab was administered for 7 Grade 1 CRS events (8.8%).
Steroids were administered concurrently with 9 Grade 1 CRS events (11.3%), of which 2 of
the events (2.5%) also included tocilizumab administration.

•

Of 37 Grade 2 ASTCT CRS events reported in 33 patients in the B11 RP2D cohort, 25 events
(67.6%) were treated with fluids, and 18 events (48.6%) were treated with low flow oxygen.
Tocilizumab was administered for 10 Grade 2 CRS events (27.0 %). Steroids were
administered concurrently with 14 Grade 2 CRS events (37.8%), of which 4 of the events
(10.8%) were treated with both steroids and tocilizumab.

•

Of 5 Grade 3 ASTCT CRS events in 5 patients, 4 events were treated with a single
vasopressor, and 2 events were treated with high-flow oxygen (the other 3 events were
treated with low flow oxygen). Tocilizumab was administered for 4 of the 5 Grade 3 CRS
events (80%), which included all 4 events treated with single vasopressor. Steroids were
administered concurrently with 4 Grade 3 CRS events (80%), of which 3 of the events were
treated with both steroids and tocilizumab.

•

A single Grade 4 ASTCT CRS event required treatment with multiple vasopressors. The
patient also received tocilizumab and concurrent steroids.

The overall frequency and severity of CRS events between the B11 RP2D cohort, B11 FL RP2D
cohort, and overall Group B were generally similar. One difference noted was the frequency of
CRS events following the C1D15 mosunetuzumab infusion, which was slightly higher in the B11
RP2D cohort (when the 60 mg dose was administered; 33.3% by ASTCT 2019 grade) compared
to overall Group B (24.4% by ASTCT 2019 grade), driven mainly by a slightly higher incidence of
serious CRS events of Grade ≥2 severity.
The Applicant’s Position:
Consistent with the mechanism of action of mosunetuzumab, CRS is a serious safety concern
that may result in life-threatening consequences if not managed appropriately. To mitigate the
risk of CRS, comprehensive product labelling is proposed (see table of adverse reactions,
Warnings and Precautions and Dosage and Administration sections of the prescribing
(b) (4)
information), as well as
enhanced patient labeling in the form of a voluntary non-REMS program Medication Guide (as
(b) (4)
per 21 CFR 208)
. The primary aims of these materials are to educate

healthcare providers (HCPs) on early recognition and management of CRS, and patients on early
recognition and presentation to healthcare facilities.
The FDA’s Assessment:
CRS events were reported and graded according to the Lee 2014 grading criteria. CRS events
were also summarized by the ASTCT 2019 grading criteria. The FDA’s review is focused on CRS
events per ASTCT criteria.
All patients received prophylaxis for CRS with corticosteroid premedication one hour prior to
each mosunetuzumab dose during Cycle 1 and 2 (i.e., dexamethasone 20 mg intravenously or
methylprednisolone 80 mg administered intravenously). Corticosteroid premedication became
optional for Cycle 3 and beyond unless the patient experienced a CRS event. Oral
acetaminophen or paracetamol and/or diphenhydramine were administered per institutional
standard.
Hospitalization Requirements
Administration of mosunetuzumab occurred in a clinical setting with immediate access to
intensive care, in addition to neurology and nephrology consultation services on Study
GO29781. For the dose escalation portion of Study GO29781, patients received inpatient
monitoring through 72 hours after any mosunetuzumab dose that exceeded a previously tested
dose level. Following the dose escalation portion, hospitalization was no longer required unless
clinically indicated at the study investigator’s discretion and in consultation with the Medical
Monitor. Patients were, however, observed for at least 90 minutes after completion of each
infusion. Therefore, the data from the B11 RP2D cohort represent IV administration of
mosunetuzumab in an outpatient healthcare setting with post-infusion monitoring and access
to intensive care. Of note, per protocol version 12, for patients that experience Grade 1 or 2
CRS, hospitalization was to be considered for the next dose of mosunetuzumab. For patients
that experienced Grade 3 CRS and were able to receive subsequent treatment, hospitalization
was mandatory for the next dose.
CRS Incidence and Severity
In the B11 RP2D cohort per ASTCT criteria, 39% of patients (86/218) experienced a CRS event,
with 21% (45/218) considered serious, primarily due to the need for hospitalization. Of the 86
patients, the most frequently reported CRS signs and symptoms included pyrexia (100%), chills
(36%), hypotension (35%), tachycardia (24%), hypoxia (22%), and headache (16%). The majority
of patients experienced Grade 1 or Grade 2 CRS at 28% (61/218) and 15% (33/218),
respectively. Two percent of patients (5/218) experienced grade 3 CRS with only one patient
(<1%) experiencing grade 4 CRS. Most patients (33%) experienced CRS after the Cycle 1, Day 15
dose. The median duration of CRS was 3 days (range: 1 to 29 days), with 75% of events lasting 5
days or less. Recurrent CRS occurred in 11% of patients.
The incidence of CRS by dose was Cycle 1 Day 1 at 15% (33/218), Cycle 1 Day 8 at 5% (10/207),
150
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
Cycle 1 Day 15 at 33% (67/201), Cycle 2 Day 1 at 4% (9/198), and Cycle 3 Day 1 at 1% (2/167).
The Grade 3 CRS events occurred on Cycle 1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1. The one Grade 4 CRS
event occurred on Cycle 1 Day 15. The time to onset was shortest with the first dose of
mosunetuzumab and progressively increased with subsequent doses. The median time to onset
on Cycle 1 Day 1 was 5 hours (range: 1 to 73 hours) and Cycle 1 Day 15 was 25 hours (range: 0.1
to 391 hours). See the table below for an overview of CRS in the FL cohort and the B11 RP2D
cohort.
Neurologic adverse events occurring concurrently with CRS were observed in 16% (14/86) of
patients in the B11 RP2D cohort. The most common neurologic manifestations included
headache, confusional state, and anxiety. Additionally, increased liver function tests occurring
concurrently with CRS was observed in 3% (3/86) of patients, primarily as AST/ALT increased.
The incidence of CRS per ASTCT in the B11 FL cohort was slightly higher (44%) than in the B11
RP2D cohort, with 23% of events being serious CRS events. The pattern of most frequently
reported signs and symptoms of CRS was similar to the B11 RP2D cohort except for a higher
incidence of headache (28%). A greater proportion of patients experienced CRS events with
Cycle 1 Day 1 (23%, 21/90) than was observed in the B11 RP2D cohort at 15%. The frequency of
CRS events with Cycle 1 Day 15 was similar in both cohorts (36% and 33%, respectively).
Recurrent CRS occurred in 21% of patients (19/90) in the B11 FL cohort. Concurrent neurologic
adverse events occurred in 10% of patients and concurrent increased liver function tests were
observed in 2% in the B11 FL cohort.
Management of CRS
Per the protocol, management of CRS included interruption of mosunetuzumab (as applicable),
supportive therapy, anti-IL-6 or corticosteroid therapy, and considerations for the next dose of
mosunetuzumab. Tocilizumab and corticosteroids could be considered for prolonged Grade 1
CRS (>2 days) in patients with significant symptoms and/or comorbidities per investigator
discretion. For patients with Grade 2 CRS, tocilizumab was to be considered followed by
corticosteroids if refractory hypotension after 1-2 doses of tocilizumab. Administration of
tocilizumab and corticosteroids were prespecified for Grade 3 and Grade 4 CRS.
The management of CRS in patients in the B11 FL cohort and B11 RP2D cohort was similar
except for the use of tocilizumab without corticosteroids, which was used in 18% of patients
with CRS in the B11 FL cohort and 24% of patients with CRS in the B11 RP2D cohort (Table 41
and Table 42). The use of corticosteroids with or without tocilizumab, IV fluids, pressors,
oxygen, and intensive care were consistent across the populations. Since the use of tocilizumab
and/or corticosteroids were not prespecified for Grade 1 or Grade 2 CRS, there was
heterogeneity in the patient cases that received tocilizumab versus those who did not receive
tocilizumab, precluding any meaningful interpretation of the data. Further, all cases of CRS
resolved and the outcomes in patients treated with supportive therapy, corticosteroids,
tocilizumab, or the combination of corticosteroids and tocilizumab were descriptively similar.

Risk Mitigation
Based on the CRS profile with mosunetuzumab, a box warning for CRS is warranted along with a
corresponding medication guide. This is based on the occurrence of serious and life-threatening
cases of CRS observed and the need for providers to be accurately aware of the risk.
Additionally, the USPI includes the statement that mosunetuzumab should only be
administered by a qualified healthcare professional with appropriate medical support to
manage severe reactions such as cytokine release syndrome. The management of CRS in
relation to mosunetuzumab will be included in the USPI.
(b) (4)

8.2.4.2 Infections
Data:
Infection AEs (broadly defined under the SOC of Infections and Infestations) were reported in
46.8% patients in the B11 RP2D cohort. The majority of infections were Grade 1−2 severity.
Grade ≥3 infections were reported in 15.1% of patients. The most frequently reported Grade 3
event (in ≥2% of patients) was pneumonia (5 patients [2.3%]). Grade 4 events were sepsis (3
patients [1.4%]) and Epstein-Barr viremia and septic shock (1 patient [0.5%] each). Grade 5
infections of pneumonia and sepsis were reported in 1 patient (0.5%) each (see Table 27).
Serious infections were reported in 37 patients (17.0%), with pneumonia (7 patients [3.2%])
being the most frequent.
The majority of serious infections did not appear to occur in the context of neutropenia. In the
B11 RP2D cohort, four serious infections, enterocolitis bacterial (Grade 3), herpes zoster
154
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
(Grade 2), and two events of sepsis (Grade 5 and Grade 4) occurred concurrently with
neutropenia/neutrophil count decreased events.
At the CCOD, 76 patients (34.9%) had all events resolved and the remaining 26 patients (11.9%)
had at least one unresolved or ongoing event.
The frequencies of infection events and serious events were similar between the B11 RP2D and
the B11 FL RP2D cohorts, and Group B overall.
The Applicant’s Position:
Serious infections are anticipated with mosunetuzumab administration due to its mode of
action resulting in B-cell depletion. Based on the increased risk of infections associated with
mosunetuzumab, serious infections are included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the
product labelling with guidance not to administer mosunetuzumab in the presence of active
infections and to consider prophylactic antibacterial, antiviral and/or antifungal medications, as
appropriate. Certain infections (e.g., upper respiratory tract infections, urinary tract infection,
and pneumonia) are included as clinically important adverse reactions in the prescribing
information.
FDA Assessment:
In general, the FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The FDA agrees with the reported 47%
incidence of infections in the B11 RP2D cohort (N = 218) of which 17% were serious infections,
including pneumonia in 5% of patients, sepsis in 3%, and urinary tract infection and upper
respiratory infection in 2% each. The 14% incidence of Grade 3 or 4 infections in the B11 RP2D
cohort was similarly driven by pneumonia (3%), sepsis (3%), and upper respiratory infections
(2%). Two patients in the B11 RP2D cohort died due to pneumonia and sepsis, respectively. The
incidence of infections in the B11 FL cohort (N = 90) at 51% (46/90) was slightly higher than that
observed in the B11 RP2D cohort. Twenty percent (18/90) of patients experienced a serious
infection, including urinary tract infections in 4% of patients, sepsis in 3% of patients, and
COVID-19, Epstein-Barr viremia, and pneumonia in 2% of patients each. The incidence of Grade
3 or 4 infections was 17% (15/90), most often due to pneumonia (3%), sepsis (3%), COVID-19
(2%), and upper respiratory infections (2%). There were no fatal infections observed in the FL
cohort. Infections remained unresolved in 10% of patients (9/90) in the B11 FL cohort at the
time of the data cutoff.
Based on the risk of serious, severe, and fatal infections associated with mosunetuzumab, the
FDA agrees with inclusion of a Warning and Precaution for infections in the USPI.
8.2.4.3 Tumor Flare
Data:
155
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
Tumor flare events were identified by PT of tumor flare, as suspected tumor flare events, or by
AESI Grade ≥2 tumor flare/tumor inflammation specific for mosunetuzumab.
A total of 9/218 patients (4.1%) in the B11 RP2D cohort experienced events that met the
definition of tumor flare events following initial treatment with mosunetuzumab.
The reported tumor flare events included tumor flare and pleural effusion (4 patients [1.8%]
each), and tumor inflammation (1 patient [0.5%]). All reported events were of Grade 2 or
Grade 3 maximum severity. Grade 3 events were tumor flare (3 patients [1.4%]) and pleural
effusion (2 patients [0.9%]). Serious tumor flare events were reported in 4 patients (1.9%). The
median time to onset of first tumor flare events was 13 days (range: 5−84 days), with median
duration of 10 days (range: 1−77 days). At the CCOD, the majority of the events (8 of 9 events
[88.9%]) had resolved.
Similar frequencies of tumor flare events and serious tumor flare events were noted between
the B11 RP2D and the B11 FL RP2D cohorts and Group B overall. Further details for tumor flare
events are provided in the SCS, Section 2.7.6.
The Applicant’s Position:
Consistent with the mechanism of action, tumor flare is likely due to the rapid influx of T cells to
tumor sites and can result in enlargement and mass effect compromising surrounding
structures. Since there is the potential for clinically significant impact based on compromise of
anatomical site of lesions secondary to mass effect, tumor flare is listed as a clinically important
adverse reaction and included in the Warnings and Precautions section of the product labelling
with monitoring and management advice to allow prescribers to plan and manage patients
appropriately.
FDA Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position and the data presented above. The incidence of
grade 2 and greater tumor flare in the B11 RP2D cohort and B11 FL cohort were 4% (9/218) and
3% (3/90), respectively. Only one tumor flare adverse event in the RP2D B11 cohort had not
resolved by the clinical cutoff date.
The FDA agrees with the Applicant that the risk of tumor flare and the potential for airway
compromise or organ impairment secondary to mass effect is clinically important. Because of
the risk, the FDA agrees with inclusion of tumor flare as a Warning and Precaution for
mosunetuzumab in the USPI.

of HLH remains unclear. The patient died due to progressive disease along with ongoing
HLH.
2. Dose Escalation Group A (2.8 mg every 3 weeks): A 74 year old Asian female with
Richter’s transformation received mosunetuzumab through Cycle 2 Day 1 (2 doses). The
patient developed Grade 2 HLH on study Day 58 based on high ferritin and low
fibrinogen along with a constellation of signs and symptoms that started on study Day
15. The patient developed septic shock, electrolyte imbalance, and seizure as part of
HLH. With the evaluation for HLH, a prior marrow aspirate and lymph node biopsy
showed concurrent EBV infection, which possibly contributed to the development of
HLH. The patient ultimately died due to HLH on study Day 60.
3. Dose Escalation Group B (20 mg every 3 weeks, step-up doses): A 67 year old White
male with Richter’s transformation received mosunetuzumab for the complete
treatment regimen of 17 cycles. Approximately 46 days following the last dose of
mosunetuzumab, the patient developed CMV and EBV infections and a bone marrow
biopsy was consistent with HLH, along with correlative laboratory findings such as high
ferritin. The patient was treated with etoposide and dexamethasone for HLH. The
patients HLH was resolving with treatment, however, the patient died due to renal
failure around 9 months following his last dose of mosunetuzumab.
4. Dose escalation Group B (6 mg every 3 weeks, step-up doses): A 72 year old White male
with small lymphocytic lymphoma (SLL) received mosunetuzumab through Cycle 1 Day
15 (3 doses). The patient died due to progressive disease on study Day 26. Upon
autopsy, the cause of death was multi-organ failure due to HLH and chronic active EBV
infection with progressive SLL. The relationship with EBV infection and HLH in the
setting of progressive disease remains unclear.
The cases of HLH raise concern regarding an association of HLH with mosunetuzumab, however
the relationship remains unclear at this time. Further, the contribution of EBV viremia also
remains unclear. However, based on the mechanism of action of mosunetuzumab, there is a
potential risk for potentiation of infections such as EBV and CMV and the development of HLH.
Continued prospective evaluation of HLH in ongoing trials evaluating mosunetuzumab is
warranted to further characterize the risk of HLH with mosunetuzumab. Additionally, a PMR to
evaluate safety with longer term use of mosunetuzumab will be issued and HLH will be included
in the assessment.
8.2.4.6 Neurologic toxicity
FDA Assessment:
Neurologic toxicity was identified as an adverse event of special interest by the Applicant for
mosunetuzumab and monitored and evaluated in Study GO29791. On Study GO29781, all
patients were required to undergo a baseline complete neurologic exam prior to the first
administration of mosunetuzumab. If new or worsening neurologic toxicity occurred, it was

Reference ID: 5100050
