recommended that the patient be referred to a neurologist and corticosteroids would
potentially be considered for treatment. Imaging was performed as clinically indicated. Per the
protocol, neurologic adverse events (NAE) were defined as adverse events that mapped to
either the system organ class (SOC) Nervous System Disorders or Psychiatric disorders. Based
on this, 56% of patients in the B11 RP2D (N = 218) cohort experienced at least one neurologic
adverse event as compared to 69% of patients in the B11 FL cohort (N = 90) as shown in Table
43 below. The majority of NAEs were Grade 1 and 2 in both cohorts, with 5% of events being
Grade 3 in the B11 RP2D cohort and 4% being Grade 3 in the B11 FL cohort. Serious NAEs were
observed in 6% in the B11 RP2D cohort and 4% in the B11 FL cohort. Twenty-one percent of
events in both cohorts were unresolved at the time of data cutoff (August 2021).
The Applicant also identified a subset of NAEs with preferred terms that could potentially
impact cognition, consciousness, and the ability to drive, i.e., driving-impacting cognition or
consciousness neurologic adverse event (DI-CCNAE), including but not limited to: amnesia,
aphasia, confusional state, delirium, depressed level of consciousness, disturbance in attention,
encephalopathy, hallucination, hepatic encephalopathy, insomnia, memory impairment,
seizure, visual hallucination, and vertigo. Using this definition, 12% of patients in the B11 RP2D
cohort and 10% of patients in the B11 FL cohort experienced DI-CCNAEs, respectively. The
majority were Grade 1 or 2, with 3% being Grade 3 in the B11 RP2D cohort. The most common
events in the B11 RP2D cohort were confusional state (4%), fatigue (2%), and neurotoxicity
(1%).
In the evaluation of neurotoxicity, the FDA was able to confirm the results provided by the
Applicant using the definition of adverse events that mapped to the Nervous System Disorder
and Psychiatric SOCs and the definition of DI-CCNAE. To further evaluate neurologic toxicity, the
FDA performed analyses using the definitions below, which includes the preferred terms
observed in the respective safety populations for mosunetuzumab:
1. Central Neurologic Toxicity
a. Mental Status Change: amnesia, cognitive disorder, confusional state, delirium,
depressed level of consciousness, disturbance in attention, memory impairment,
somnolence, encephalopathy, hallucination
b. Aphasia, ataxia, balance disorder, dizziness, dyskinesia, gait disturbance,
neurotoxicity, seizure, syncope, toxic neuropathy, tremor
2. Peripheral Neuropathy
a. Burning sensation, dysaesthesia, hyperaesthesia, hypoaesthesia, neuralgia,
paraesthesia, peripheral neuropathy, peripheral motor neuropathy, peripheral
sensory neuropathy
NAE due to Nervous System Disorders in the B11 RP2D Cohort
In the B11 RP2D cohort, neurologic adverse events under the nervous system disorders SOC
occurred in 39% of patients, with 3% (7/218) having Grade 3 events. There were no Grade 4

neurologic events. Grade 1 neurologic TEAEs occurred in 35% of patients (77/218), Grade 2 in
7% (16/218), and Grade 3 in 3% (7/218). The most common manifestations of NAE due to
nervous system disorders were headache (21%), peripheral neuropathy (13%), and dizziness

disorder was unresolved at the time of data cutoff and could be largely attributed to the
incidence of peripheral neuropathy in 7% (6/90) and headache in 6% (5/90) of patients.
NAE due to Psychiatric System Disorders in the B11 RP2D Cohort
Neurologic adverse events under the psychiatric system disorders SOC occurred in 19% of
patients in the B11 RP2D cohort. Grade 1 events occurred in 12% of patients (27/218) and
grade 2 events in 7% (7/218). The single Grade 3 event of confusional state under psychiatric
system disorder SOC was included in the analysis of mental status change described above. The
most common manifestations in the B11 RP2D cohort were insomnia (11%) and anxiety (3%).

Unresolved NAE in B11 RP2D
At the time of the safety data cutoff (August 2021), 45 patients (21%) had unresolved NAEs.
Based on updated information provided from the Applicant using a data cutoff of July 8, 2022,
16 patients (7%) died with unresolved NAEs (12 due to progression of disease, 2 due to
infectious adverse events, and 2 unknown), 4 patients (2%) withdrew from the study with
unresolved NAEs, 17 patients (8%) still had unresolved NAEs, and 8 patients (4%) had resolving
NAEs. The most common unresolved NAEs remained insomnia, peripheral neuropathy, and
headache.
Conclusion:
Sixty-nine percent of patients in the B11 FL cohort and 56% of patients in the B11 RP2D cohort
experienced at least one neurologic adverse event. The majority of the neurologic adverse
events were grade 1 and grade 2. However, serious events occurred in 6% and Grade 3 severe
events occurred in 5%. In addition, 21% of patients had unresolved NAEs at the data cutoff. The
signs and symptoms of neurologic toxicity included headache, peripheral neuropathy, dizziness,
and mental status change (including confusional state, disturbance in attention, cognitive
disorder, delirium, encephalopathy, and somnolence). Two cases of ICANS were also observed.
The most common psychiatric manifestation were insomnia and anxiety. Given the risk of
neurologic toxicity, labeling should include neurologic toxicity as a Warning and Precaution,
including driving restrictions, to ensure safe use of mosunetuzumab.

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
The Applicant’s Position:
It is expected that safety issues can be adequately managed through labeling, additional risk
minimization measures and routine postmarketing surveillance.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. In the postmarket setting, the Applicant will be
required to complete an integrated safety analysis in patients with follicular lymphoma and
other lymphoid malignancies to further characterize the incidence and severity of neurologic
adverse reactions, hematologic adverse reactions, hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis,
infections, and pneumonitis/interstitial lung disease, that may develop with longer term use of
mosunetuzumab.

Integrated Assessment of Safety
The Applicant’s Position:
The overall safety profile of mosunetuzumab at the RP2D/intended registration dose appears
manageable with no unexpected safety signals, considering the advanced nature of the disease
and the heavily pretreated patient population under study. The AE profile consisted of CRS
(majority were Grade 1-2, limited to Cycle 1, manageable and all resolved); hematologic and
associated laboratory abnormalities (limited clinical significance, manageable and reversible);
and low rates of treatment discontinuation. The current pharmacovigilance plan and product
label include guidance for patient management of CRS, serious infections, tumor flare, and TLS,
and these measures are considered adequate to manage the risks. The tolerability of
mosunetuzumab was evident by the low proportion of patients who discontinued
mosunetuzumab due to AEs. Most AEs requiring intervention could be adequately managed
and resolved following dose interruptions (withholding of dose), and/or supportive care. Dose
interruptions generally did not prevent patients continuing to receive their planned
mosunetuzumab dose for the majority of the duration of treatment.
The mosunetuzumab safety profile demonstrates advantages over other approved treatments
for R/R FL ≥2 Prior Therapies. The treatment discontinuation rate due to AEs (4.1%) is lower
compared to PI3K inhibitors (15-35%) or tazemetostat (8%) (see Table 4 of Clinical Overview,
Module 2.5), indicating good tolerability. The CRS profile appears to be milder than CAR-T
therapy. Compared to axicabtagene ciloleucel, CRS events reported for mosunetuzumab were
less frequent and less severe with fewer Grade ≥3 CRS, and the absence of fatal CRS events.
Mosunetuzumab also compares favorably with axicabtagene ciloleucel with respect to
neurotoxicity profile with a lower incidence and severity of neurologic adverse events, including
Grade ≥3 and serious events with no fatal neurologic adverse events. Mosunetuzumab also has
other advantages over axicabtagene ciloleucel, being immediately available for patients,

supportive.

Conclusions and Recommendations

The FDA’s Assessment:
The benefit-risk assessment is favorable for mosunetuzumab, a bispecific CD20 directed CD3 T
cell engager indicated for the treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular
lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy.
Efficacy:
Efficacy of mosunetuzumab is based on the results from GO29781, an open-label, multicenter,
multicohort study in 90 patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma who had
received at least two prior therapies, including an anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and an
alkylating agent.
Patients received step-up doses of 1 mg on Cycle 1 Day 1 and 2 mg on Cycle 1 Day 8, followed
by 60 mg on Cycle 1 Day 15, and 60 mg on Cycle 2 Day 1, then 30 mg every 3 weeks in
subsequent cycles. A treatment cycle was 21 days. Mosunetuzumab was administered for 8
cycles unless patients experienced progressive disease or unacceptable toxicity. After 8 cycles,
patients with a complete response discontinued therapy; patients with a partial response or
stable disease continued treatment up to 17 cycles, unless patients experienced progressive
disease or unacceptable toxicity.
Efficacy was established on the basis of objective response rate and duration of response as
assessed by an independent review facility according to standard criteria for NHL (Cheson
2007). Among the 90 patients, the ORR per IRF was 80% (95% CI: 70, 88), with 60% achieving a
complete response. With a median follow-up for DOR of 14.9 months, the estimated median
DOR was 22.8 months (95% CI: 10, not reached). Of the 72 responders, 62% remained in
response at 12 months and 57% at 18 months.
Safety:
The safety profile of mosunetuzumab was primarily supported by analysis of 218 patients with
B-cell NHL that received the recommended dose and regimen (1/2/60/30). The median
duration of treatment was 8 cycles (range 1 to 17), with 52% exposed for at least 8 cycles. The
most common adverse events (≥20%) were cytokine release syndrome (39%), fatigue (36%),
rash (34%), pyrexia (24%), and headache (21%). The most common Grade 3 to 4 laboratory
abnormalities (≥ 10%) were decreased lymphocyte count (92%), decreased phosphate (41%),
increased glucose (40%), decreased neutrophil count (38%), increased uric acid (15%),
decreased white blood cell count (22%), decreased hemoglobin (19%), and decreased platelets
(12%). Serious adverse events occurred in 52%, most often due to CRS (21%), pneumonia (5%),
pyrexia (5%), sepsis (3%), and a 2% incidence each of tumor flare, urinary tract infection, pleural
effusion, and renal insufficiency. Adverse events leading to treatment interruption occurred in
175
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
33%, most often due to neutropenia, infection, and CRS. Adverse events leading to treatment
discontinuation occurred in 4%, most often due to CRS. Thus, the primary safety issues
identified with mosunetuzumab include CRS, neurologic toxicity (including ICANS), infections,
cytopenias, and tumor flare.
Benefit-Risk:
Mosunetuzumab has an overall favorable benefit-risk in patients with relapsed or refractory
follicular lymphoma after two or more lines of systemic therapy. In the 90 patients with
relapsed or refractory FL enrolled in the GO29781 study, the median age was 60 years (range:
29 to 90 years), 33% were 65 years of age or older, 61% were male, 82% were White, 9% were
Asian, 4% were Black or African American, and 8% were Hispanic or Latino. A total of 77% of
patients had Stage III-IV disease, 34% had bulky disease, and all patients had an Eastern
Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) performance status of 0 or 1. The median number of prior
therapies was 3 (range: 2 to 10), with 38% receiving 2 prior therapies, 31% receiving 3 prior
therapies, and 31% receiving more than 3 prior therapies.
In this population, the overall response rate of 80%, with 60% achieving complete response,
with durability and a tolerable safety profile constitutes a meaningful clinical benefit and a
favorable benefit-risk evaluation. To support accelerated approval, the efficacy data are
considered in the context of available therapy for the patients with relapsed or refractory
follicular lymphoma in the 3rd line setting and beyond. Available therapy includes anti-CD20
therapy (i.e., rituximab and obinutuzumab) as a single agent and in combination, bendamustine
monotherapy and in combination, and immunotherapy combinations such as rituximab plus
lenalidomide. In the 3rd line setting and beyond, the ORRs for these therapies, including singleagent and combinations, ranges from 57% to 80% with associated durability. In the 90 patients
with FL treated with mosunetuzumab, all patients received prior anti-CD20 monoclonal
antibody and an alkylating agent and the median number of prior therapies was 3. A total of
79% of patients were refractory to prior anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody therapy, 53% were
refractory to both anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody and alkylator therapy, 9% received prior
rituximab plus lenalidomide therapy, 21% received prior autologous stem cell transplant, and
3% received prior CAR T therapy. Therefore, the overall response of 80% with associated
durability for mosunetuzumab provides data to support a clinically meaningful benefit in the
context of available therapy for patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma in the
3rd line setting and beyond.
Thus, the benefit-risk of mosunetuzumab is deemed favorable to support accelerated approval
for the following indication:
• Treatment of adult patients with relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma after two or
more lines of systemic therapy

14 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The FDA’s Assessment:
The review team recommends two postmarketing requirements (PMR), an accelerated
approval PMR to verify clinical benefit and a safety PMR for further characterization of safety
with longer-term use of mosunetuzumab.
1) Conduct a randomized clinical trial in patients with relapsed or refractory follicular
lymphoma, with patients randomized to receive mosunetuzumab in combination with
lenalidomide or rituximab in combination with lenalidomide. The primary endpoint
should be progression-free survival, with secondary endpoints that include response
rate and overall survival. The trial should enroll a sufficiently representative study
population to reflect the racial and ethnic diversity of the U.S. patient population with
follicular lymphoma and allow for interpretation of the results in these patient
populations.

infections, and pneumonitis/interstitial lung disease, that may develop with longer term
use of mosunetuzumab. Include patient-level and pooled analyses of ongoing and

OCP Appendices (Technical documents supporting OCP

Summary of Bioanalytical Method
The FDA’s Assessment:
Mosunetuzumab concentrations in human serum were determined using a validated enzymelinked immunosorbent assay (ELISA).
In the assay, a monoclonal anti-idiotypic antibody against the anti-CD20 arm of
mosunetuzumab was used for capture. A biotin-conjugated monoclonal anti-idiotypic antibody
against the anti-CD3 arm of mosunetuzumab was applied for detection. Streptavidin (SA)horseradish peroxidase (HRP) and a peroxidase substrate (tetramethyl benzidine) were added
sequentially for color development. The lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) of the assay is 10
ng/mL. This assay was validated at two sites for analyzing samples from Study GO29781. Results
for the methods validation, cross-validation and method performance during study sample
analysis are provided in Table 52 and Table 53 below.

Mosunetuzumab Pharmacodynamics
The FDA’s Assessment:
B-cell Depletion
To assess the magnitude and kinetics of B-cell depletion, a quantitative flow cytometry assay to
detect CD19+ B cells was added to the Study Protocol (version 9 [dated 30 January2019]). B-cell
depletion was measured in 152 patients from cohort B11. A majority (110/152 patients, 72%) of
patients enrolled had B-cell counts below 70 cells/ul (the lower limit of the normal range for
peripheral B cell counts) at baseline, and approximately half of patients (71/152, 47%) enrolled
had B-cell counts below the level of quantitation (LLOQ) for the assay (5 cells/ul) at baseline.
For patients with paired baseline and on-study samples, i.e., those having measurable B-cells
(>=5 cells/ul) prior to mosunetuzumab dosing and at least one additional measurement posttreatment, B-cells were found to be reduced to undetectable levels in most patients within
Cohort B11 as early as Cycle 2 [58/63 (92%)], and depletion was sustained at later cycles
including at Cycle 4 (58/60, 97%), and Cycle 8 (45/48, 94%)] (Table 58). Furthermore, 100% of
these patients had B-cell depletion to <70 cells/ul by cycle 4.

IgG Levels
Of the 105 evaluable patients in the B11 RP2D cohort (n=218) who had a baseline IgG
measurement and at least one post-baseline IgG measurement, a total of 38 patients enrolled
had IgG levels <5 g/L, the remaining 67 patients had baseline IgG levels ≥ 5 g/L.
• Among 38 patients with baseline IgG levels <5 g/L, 78.9% (30/38) of the patients
maintained post-baseline IgG levels <5 g/L.
• Among 67 patients with baseline IgG levels ≥5 g/L, 59.7% (40/67) of the patients
maintained post-baseline IgG levels ≥5 g/L, while the remaining patients (27/67; 40.3%)
had the IgG values decreased to <5 g/L.
202
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
with R/R NHL. The Applicant’s final PopPK models of mosunetuzumab is characterized as 2compartment PPK model with time varying clearance, which is adequate to describe the
mosunetuzumab serum concentration-time profiles of R/R FL patients, following the IV
administration of mosunetuzumab with 1/2/60/30 mg Q3W. The shrinkage value of the
Applicant’s mosunetuzumab PopPK model was 6.69% for IIV on CL, 6.64% for IIV on V1, 22.8%
for V2, and 32.1% for distributional clearance Q, Inter-individual variabilities (IIV) were
estimated to be higher for CLbase (63%) and half-life HLtrans (86%) versus CLss (18%), V1 (31%),
and V2 (25%); Shrinkage was relatively low on CLbase, V1, and CLss, supporting a robust
covariate analysis on these parameters, and indicating there was no obvious bias in the
parameter estimates for mosunetuzumab.
Covariate analysis results showed the statistically significant covariates e.g., body weight, tumor
burden, albumin, baseline anti-CD20 drug concentrations (specifically residual rituximab [R] or
Gazyva [G]), and sex had a marginal impact on mosunetuzumab pharmacokinetics when
assessing the influence of extreme values. In addition, neither patient age, renal impairment
status, hepatic impairment status, drug material used, histology (i.e., FL vs. DLBCL vs. other),
nor race (Asian vs. non-Asian) were identified as covariates influencing mosunetuzumab PK.
Given the unique step-up dose regimen, and time dependent clearance, additional simulations
including PopPK model–predicted mosunetuzumab concentration–time profiles, receptor
occupancy (RO)-time (%), and CL–time (L/day) profiles based on the 1/2/60/30 mg dose
regimen were performed to support the AUC endpoints selected, and E-R analysis for CRS. The
final PK model is acceptable for simulating post-hoc exposure metrics of mosunetuzumab
(AUC0-42, AUC0-63, AUC0-84) and RO(%) used for subsequent E-R analyses for efficacy and safety
measurements.
In general, the Applicant’s PopPK analysis for mosunetuzumab is considered acceptable for the
purpose of supporting analyses objectives. The Applicant’s analyses were verified by the
reviewer, with no significant discordance identified. More specifically, the developed model is
acceptable to be used to derive exposure metrics for E-R analysis, and to support the
Applicant’s proposed labeling statements about general PK information including intrinsic and
extrinsic factors in the current submission.

Special Population
Renal impairment
The covariate analysis results showed that CrCL was not statistically significant covariates on
mosunetuzumab CL. The impact of the degree of renal impairment on mosunetuzumab CL was
further assessed by categorizing patients with varying degrees of renal impairment into 4
categories based on their estimated CrCL (Normal: CrCL> 90 mL/min, Mild: 60 to < 90 mL/min,
Moderate: 30 to <60 mL/min, and Severe: CrCL <30 mL/min). There is marginally increased AUC
in patients with moderate and severe renal impairment compared to mild renal impairment
and normal renal function (Figure 36). The Applicant claimed that the PK exposure is not
expected to be impacted by renal impairment.
Figure 36: Comparison of AUC0-42 in Renal Function Groups at 1/2/60/30 mg

Receptor Occupancy (RO) for Mosunetuzumab
Due to varying levels of residual rituximab and/or obinutuzumab, especially in patients with
aNHL, the mosunetuzumab receptor occupancy (%RO) has been used to address the
confounding effect of the ER relationship for efficacy and cytokine release syndrome (CRS). The
observed mosunetuzumab CD20 %RO time–course was calculated within the NONMEM using
Equation below:

225
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}

Here, Mosun(t), Ritux(t), and Obinut(t) are the model–predicted time–courses (ng/mL) for mosunetuzumab,
rituximab, and obinutuzumab, respectively. KD,Mosun (10200 ng/mL), KD,Ritux (675 ng/mL), and KD,Obinut (600
ng/mL) are the equilibrium dissociation constants for mosunetuzumab, rituximab and obinutuzumab, respectively,
per Scatchard assay data on file.

The observed average mosunetuzumab CD20 receptor occupancy over 42 days (ROavg0-42)
was calculated within the NONMEM code by implementing a reservoir compartment in which
receptor occupancy accumulated over time, denoted by the cumulative area under the
receptor occupancy time curve (AUC_RO). The average %RO was then calculated by
AUC_RO/time for any selected time point.
The PopPK model–predicted mosunetuzumab concentration–time (ug/mL), receptor occupancy
(RO)-time (%), and CL–time (L/day) profiles based on the cohort B11 (N=214) from the popPK
model estimates for the 1/2/60/30 mg dose regimen together with model-predicted rituximab
concentration–time (ug/mL) profiles are shown in Figure 38. Given the unique step-up dosing
regimen, and time dependent clearance, these figures were provided to illustrate the
complexities of this PK system, and to support the AUC endpoints selected. As shown, CL
decreases with time, from a typical CLbase of ~1.08 L/day to a CLss of ~0.584 L/day. Given the
typical HLtrans of 16.3 days, 90% of steady state will be reached after approximately 3.3 halflives, i.e., 54 days; this is confirmed by the blue line representing the typical mosunetuzumab
concentration for the dosing regimen of 1/2/60/30 mg.
Figure 38: Model–Predicted Mosunetuzumab Concentration– (blue),RO-(grey), CL– (red), and
Rituximab Concentration–Time (green) Profiles for 1/2/60/30 mg Dosing Regimen Based on
Cohort B11 Patient EBEs (N=214)

Exposure-Response Analysis
20.4.5.1. ER (efficacy) Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
In support of the current BLA application, the Applicant submitted exposure-response (E-R) for
efficacy analysis including complete response rate (CRR), objective response rate (ORR) and PFS
with R/R NHL patients from Studies GO29781 to characterize E-R for efficacy relationships.
Under the proposed dosage of 1/2/60/30 mg, the E-R efficacy analysis indicated that clinical
responses (CR, ORR) increased with increasing mosunetuzumab exposure (AUC0–42) and
approached a plateau. Further ER analysis showed a longer median PFS with increased
227
Version date: January 2020 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
mosunetuzumab exposure (AUC0–42), and a longer median PFS at the 1/2/60/30 mg dose (B11
cohort, RP2D) compared to the 1/2/13.5 mg dose (B7 cohort). Loading doses of 60 mg on Cycle
1 Day 15 and Cycle 2 Day 1 were predicted by E-R analysis to achieve a plateau for clinical
endpoints ORR and CR .
Overall, the Applicant’s E-R analysis for efficacy appear adequate. The E-R relationship for
efficacy endpoints may imply that the effectiveness of mosunetuzumab reached a plateau at
the proposed dosing regimen of 1/2/60/30 mg, which supports the proposed dosage.
20.4.5.2. ER (efficacy) Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant’s E-R for efficacy analysis of Mosunetuzumab were conducted in 159 patients
with relapsed or refractory B-cell Follicular Lymphoma (R/R FL) patients with >2 Prior Therapies
in Study GO29781 in one of the following two dosing regimens: 1) every 3 weeks (q3w) fixed
dosing schedule (Group A) or 2) on a Cycle 1 step-up dosing regimen (Group B), followed by a
once every 3 weeks (q3w) fixed dosing schedule. The dose range for these patients was from
0.2 mg to 1/2/60/30 mg; specifically, 7 patients from Group A cohorts received doses ranging
from 0.2 to 2.8 mg q3w, and 152 patients from Group B step-up dosing cohorts received doses
ranging from 0.4/1.0/2.8 mg to 1.0/2.0/60/30 mg.
A brief overview of E-R analysis for Mosunetuzumab is listed in the Table 62: and Figure 39. The
efficacy endpoints for E-R analyses primarily included CR, OR, PFS, DOCR, and DOR in R/R FL
patients with >2 Prior Therapies. The E-R analysis for R/R FL covered age ranging from 27 to 90
years with a median of 60 years, body weight ranging from 42 to 140 kg with a median of 80.4
kg, and albumin ranging from 20 to 480 g/L with a median of 41 g/L. Table 63: shows statistical
summaries of continuous and categorical covariates for the efficacy E-R population.

20.4.5.3. ER (safety) Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:

In support of the dosage recommendations for this BLA, the Applicant also submitted reports of
exposure-response (E-R) for safety analysis from patients with R/R NHL from Studies GO29781
to characterize E-R for safety relationships. The E-R for safety results indicated that the no
positive E-R relationship for any AEs Grade ≥3 and any serious AE with PK exposure to the time
of event in the pooled population across all evaluated doses. The Applicant’s E-R for safety
analysis results using ROmax % supports the step-up dosing regimen at 1/2/60/30 mg with
observed low frequency of Grade >2 CRS. Following the step-up dosing regimen did not lead to
exposure-related increases in rates and onset time of Grade >3 neutropenia, Grade ≥3
infections or infestations, any Grade >3 AEs, or any Grade >1 AEs.

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5100050

In addition, the Applicant also conducted E-R analysis to support neurological AE (NAE) risk
mitigation through step-up dosing regimens where there was a lack of ER relationships for
frequency or onset of NAE despite dose escalation of the target doses administered on Cycle 1
Day 15, Cycle 2 Day 1, and every 3 weeks thereafter. No exposure-dependency for DI-CCNAE
(driving-impacting cognition or consciousness neurologic adverse event) in R/R NHL was
observed across the dose range tested (i.e.,0.4/1/2.8 mg to 1/2/60/30 mg) using the step-up
dosing regimen. The Applicant’s E-R for NAE analysis appear reasonable.
Overall, the Applicant’s E-R for safety analysis appear adequate to support the proposed dosing
regimen (1/2/60/30 mg).
20.4.5.4. ER (safety) Assessment Summary
The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant’s E-R for safety analysis of Mosunetuzumab (Mosunetuzumab) was conducted
with 439 patients with relapsed or refractory B-cell non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma (NHL) patients in
Study GO29781. A brief overview of E-R analysis for Mosunetuzumab is listed in the Table 62:
and Figure 39. The dose range for these patients was from 0.05 mg to 1/2/60 mg, including
1/2/60/30 mg. Adverse Events Grade >2 CRS, Grade >3 neutropenia, Grade >3 infections and
infestations, any Grade >3 event, and any Grade >1 event in R/R NHL patients were included in
E-R for safety analysis. The data included 32 patients from Group A cohorts receiving doses
ranging from 0.05 to 2.8 mg every three weeks (q3w), and 407 patients from Group B step-up
dosing cohorts receiving doses ranging from 0.4/1.0/2.8 mg to 1.0/2.0/60 mg, including
1.0/2.0/60/30 mg.
The E-R safety analysis for R/R NHL covered age ranging from 19 to 96 years with a median of
63 years, body weight ranging from 37.1 to 163 kg with a median 77.9 kg, and albumin ranging
from 20 to 480 g/L with a median of 41 g/L. Table 64: shows statistical summaries of baseline
continuous and categorical covariates for the evaluable patients in the E-R for safety (E-S)
analysis.
For safety E-R of CRS, a mosunetuzumab receptor occupancy (RO)–based exposure endpoint a ROmax% metric (instead of AUC or Cmax) was used to address the confounding issue of the
ER relationship for CRS incidence from varying levels of anti-CD20 drug concentrations
(specifically residual rituximab [R] or Gazyva [G]).

The applicant also conducted the mosunetuzumab exposure-response (ER) analyses (including
logistic regression and TTE analyses) for NAEs (defined as adverse events reported as any
primary or secondary Preferred Terms [PT] in either the System Organ Class [SOC] “Nervous

(clinical cutoff date [CCOD] of 27 Aug 2021) and DI-CCNAE (driving-impacting cognition or
consciousness neurologic adverse event), including 439 pharmacokinetic-evaluable patients
(438 non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma [NHL] patients).

curve (AUC) and CD20 receptor occupancy (RO) based metrics over pre-defined time windows
of 0-7 days, 0-14 days, and 0-42 days. A summary of common NAEs (>=2%) by preferred term is
summarized in Table 66. Overall results of the ER analyses for NAE patients in the safety
evaluable population are shown in Figure 47. For Kaplan-Meier analyses, exposures were
stratified by tertiles of observed values, the TTE analyses of any-grade NAE onset are shown in

20.4.5.5. Exposure Response Analyses for Pharmacodynamic Biomarkers
Applicant’s Position
To support dosing recommendation for this BLA, the Applicant also developed the exposureresponse (ER) relationship for pharmacodynamic biomarkers such as interferon-gamma (IFN-γ)
and IL-6 in study GO29781. The IFN-r and IL-6 were selected as the primary pharmacodynamic
(PD) biomarkers of interest for the purpose of E-R characterization as a proximal marker for
target engagement as it is produced by T-cells upon activation. As shown in Figure 50, a rapid
but transient increase in IFN-γ was observed early after the initial drug administration (i.e.,
within 30 minutes after the end of infusion) and elevated IFN-γ levels were detected up to 6
hours post end of infusion but then returned to baseline levels by 24 hours. Similar trend was
also observed for IL-6 (Figure 53).
As shown in Figure 51 and Figure 52, there is no apparent dose-response relationship between
ROmax and PD biomarker- interferon-gamma (IFN-γ). No exposure-dependent increases were
observed following later cycles of dose administration. Similar to ER analysis for IFN-γ, the ER
relationship highlighted in Figure 54 and Figure 55 for IL-6 demonstrates that maximum change
in the Log2-transformed IL-6 concentration (Log2-IL-6,CFBLmax) tended to increase modestly
with increasing ROmax within the mosunetuzumab dose range evaluated following a Cycle 1,
Day 1 dose. However, the E-R relationship following the Cycle 1, Day 15 loading dose (“3rd
Dose”) remained relatively flat, even with higher exposures achieved with the 60 mg loading
dose. Beyond the third dose in later cycles, no apparent exposure-dependent IL-6 increases
were observed following mosunetuzumab administration.
This time- and repeat dose-dependent shift (i.e., reduction) in IFN-γ/ IL-6 ER relationship was
consistent with the clinical mitigation of acute cytokine release from T cells upon repeated
ascending dosing of mosunetuzumab. IL-6 is an established and generally recognized upstream
marker of CRS. Hence, interpretations of IL-6 E-R data in this response continue to support the
effectiveness of the proposed registration 1/2/60/30 mg mosunetuzumab Cycle 1 step-up
dosing for mitigating acute cytokine-driven toxicities, where mosunetuzumab IV monotherapy
is administered with escalating Cycle 1 Day 1 (C1D1), C1D8, and C1D15 dose levels, followed by
30 mg q3w dosing on Day 1 of subsequent cycles.
Figure 50: Arithmetic Mean (+SD) of Plasma IFN-γ Concentration vs. Nominal Time and
Categorical Time Texts Following IV Administrations of Mosunetuzumab in R/R NHL

BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {BLA 761263, Lunsumio (Mosunetuzumab)}
Background
Mosunetuzumab (BTCT4465A, RO7030816) is a full-length, humanized immunoglobulin G 1
(IgG1), CD3/CD20 bispecific antibody in development for the treatment of adult patients with
relapsed or refractory follicular lymphoma (R/R FL) who have received at least two prior
systemic therapies. Mosunetuzumab is proposed to be administered for 8 cycles for patients
who achieve a complete response, and for 17 cycles for patients who achieve partial response
or have stable disease in response to the treatment. Mosunetuzumab is given every 3 weeks for
the treatment duration, in which the antibody is administered via IV infusion at 1-, 2- and 60mg on Day 1, 8 and 15 of Cycle 1 (C1D1, C1D8, C1D15), respectively, at 60-mg on Day 1 of Cycle
2 (C2D1), and at 30-mg on Day 1 of Cycle 3 and subsequent cycles.
Mosunetuzumab acts by recruitment of effector T-cells via CD3 to engage with target CD20expressing B cells, leading to T-cell activation and T-cell mediated B cell cytolysis in a target- and
dose-dependent manner (20220822-response). Cytokines including interleukin 6 (IL-6) are
known to modulate CYP activities in humans. Mosunetuzumab treatment induced transient
elevation of cytokines such as IL-6. This review focused on whether the Applicant’s PBPK
modeling is adequate to predict the magnitude and duration of drug interaction effects of
mosunetuzumab on CYP3A activity via IL-6 elevation.
Methods
All simulations were performed using the PK/PD Profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator
(Version 17, Certara, Sheffield, UK). Schemes of the PBPK simulation strategy are shown in
Figure 60, which summarizes the model development and verification of the IL-6 PBPK model,
and model applications in assessing the effect of IL-6 elevation on midazolam
pharmacokinetics.
(b) (4)
